### 2. ★★★★☆ Are global oil reserves sufficient to withstand renewed US-Iran war?

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T07:37:12+00:00
- **发生了什么**: With emergency stocks at multidecade lows, analysts say the world may have only a month or two if Hormuz shuts again.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Are global oil reserves sufficient to withstand renewed US-Iran war? on social media
Vessels are seen anchored off Bandar Abbas, Iran, along the Strait of Hormuz [File: Amirhossein Khorgooei/ISNA via AFP]
Oil prices have climbed towards their highest levels in five months as attacks on tankers in and around the Strait of Hormuz mount and efforts to end the United States-Israel war on Iran are deadlocked.
Brent crude, the international benchmark, rose by more than $2 to $110.85 a barrel on Wednesday, close to a five-month peak.
list of 4 items list 1 of 4 US-sanctioned ICC wins Sakharov Prize, EU’s top human rights award
list 2 of 4 Trump says Musk restored Ukraine’s Starlink access amid Russia war
list 3 of 4 Will ICE agents play a role in the 2026 US midterm elections?
list 4 of 4 Ilhan Omar plans to move out of her Washington DC home after federal raid
A day earlier, Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) said two tankers were “set ablaze” after hitting mines at what it called an illegal crossing point in the strait. Then, on Thursday, the IRGC said it hit seven more tankers although Washington dismissed the claim. In a statement, the IRGC said four of the vessels were transferring oil when they were targeted and caught fire overnight.
In September, the Houthis, an armed group also known as Ansar Allah (Arabic for Supporters of God), seized territory in Yemen close to the Bab al-Mandeb strait, a critical chokepoint into and out of the Red Sea and a gateway to Egypt’s Suez Canal.
The escalation came as the world’s emergency oil stocks, already tapped heavily since the Iran war began on February 28, sit at their lowest levels in decades.
Al Jazeera put a question to analysts: If fighting resumes in full, the Strait of Hormuz is shut off completely and Houthi attacks choke off Bab al-Mandeb, how long could the world’s reserves last?
Strategic oil reserves are stockpiles of crude oil and fuel held by governments or by companies on their behalf for use in a supply emergency.
The International Energy Agency (IEA), which was established in 1974 after the Arab oil embargo, coordinates most of them. Its importing members must hold at least 90 days of net imports. China and India are not members.
How much of the reserves has already been released this year?
On March 11, days after the start of the US-Israel war on Iran, the IEA’s 32 members agreed to make 400 million barrels available from emergency supplies, more than double the volume released after Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022.
The US pledged 172.2 million barrels from its Strategic Petroleum Reserve (SPR) and Japan 79.8 million, according to IEA figures.
The SPR has since fallen to 283 million barrels, its lowest level since October 1982, according to the US Department of Energy.
Much of that oil was lent rather than sold and must be returned with extra barrels, according to the US Energy Information Administration.
Far less than before the war began in February, and not all of it can be used.
Aaron Kildow, a senior crude oil analyst at Sparta Commodities, told Al Jazeera that global strategic stocks excluding China and Iran have fallen by nearly 327 million barrels since the end of December 2025 with the US, Europe and Japan accounting for about 95 percent of the draw.
He estimated that leaves only about 400 million barrels of usable reserves worldwide, excluding China’s stockpile, which is unlikely to be exported, and Gulf stocks, which could be cut off by a total closure of Hormuz.
Nearly half of these available stocks are in Japan.
Europe has about 76 million barrels uncommitted, but members such as Germany are holding some back for a local emergency, Kildow said.
“Europe is the real wildcard, both on timing and willingness,” Kildow said.
Meanwhile, China’s onshore crude stocks stand at 1.13 billion barrels, down from 1.23 billion in late February but still enough for about 80 days of refinery intake, Muyu Xu, a senior crude oil analyst at the analytics firm Kpler, told Al Jazeera.
India’s reserves cover just eight days of its imports, the Reuters news agency reported.
What would a full closure of Hormuz and Bab al-Mandeb mean?
Before the war, about one-fifth of the world’s oil and liquefied natural gas supplies, or roughly 20 million barrels of oil a day, passed through the Strait of Hormuz.
Saudi Arabia has increased the amount of crude it transports from its oilfields in the east to its Red Sea port of Yanbu in the west via the East-West Pipeline, thereby bypassing Hormuz.
But there have been strikes on that pipeline, and tankers heading south from Yanbu must pass through Bab a-Mandeb, where the Iran-backed Houthis declared a blockade on Saudi shipping in July.
Only a confirmed 373,000 barrels a day of Saudi crude have transited Bab al-Mandeb so far this month, the lowest level since February, Xu said, with most Yanbu cargoes now heading north via Egypt instead.
If Hormuz were shut and the Houthis blocked all Saudi tankers, Gulf producers could export only through a few pipelines and Yanbu’s northbound route, Xu said.
That would leave about 7.5 million barrels a day of Middle Eastern crude, against 16.8 million now, a shortfall of about 9.3 million barrels a day, she estimated.
“If such attacks occur, the supply disruption could be even more severe and prolonged,” Xu said, referring to strikes on oilfields, terminals and pipelines.
Not for long – possibly just weeks, according to experts.
If Kpler’s estimated shortfall had to be covered entirely from the roughly 400 million usable barrels that Kildow identified, they could not, in practice, be released that fast. Japanese and South Korean reserves can move quickly, Kildow said, but US barrels are staged over three months, and Europe’s contribution is uncertain.
Chris Beauchamp, chief market analyst at IG Group, said the world was fortunate after the first closure. “The salient feature of March and beyond was how a crisis didn’t actually develop. We got away with it,” he said.
“I don’t think the global economy will be as lucky next time if there is a protracted conflict,” Beauchamp said. “A month or two is all we have.”
The world would not literally run out of oil. Instead, prices would rise until enough demand disappeared. “One hundred fifty dollars [per barrel] becomes a very distinct possibility, which then sees significant demand destruction,” Beauchamp said.
The sharpest squeeze would be on diesel, which is already nearly double the price of crude, the IEA said.
Refiners would compete for crude grades rich in diesel, “a cost that ultimately lands on consumers,” Kildow said.
Most Gulf exports in September and October went to Asian buyers, and India and Pakistan are among the most exposed, Kildow said, adding that their nearest alternative supply, from West Africa, is 25 to 28 days away by sea, compared with two days from Fujairah in the United Arab Emirates.
“The squeeze won’t hit uniformly. It lands fastest and hardest where reserves are thinnest and substitute supply is farthest away,” Kildow said.
In the second quarter of this year, as the US-Iran war was under way, ExxonMobil reported earnings of $14.5bn, its highest in four years while Chevron posted $12bn, its best in six years and Shell more than doubled its earnings to nearly $10bn. Their profits are largely down to the high oil prices.
A second shock would send refiners scrambling for supplies from the US, Latin America, West Africa and Russia, Xu said.
“This would allow non-Middle Eastern producers to benefit from higher crude prices, potentially alongside lower transportation costs,” she said.
In August, US President Donald Trump said companies such as ExxonMobil and Chevron were “making too much money based on a shortage”.

</details>

### 3. ★★★★☆ Surveillance and 'constant paranoia': Western diplomats tell BBC of life in war-time Moscow

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-22T23:16:20+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Officials compare working in Russia since its full-scale invasion of Ukraine to the Cold War era.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Ex-US and French ambassadors Lynne Tracy and Pierre LÃ©vy were among those to speak to the BBC of their time in Moscow
"Being the ambassador to Russia was probably the hardest assignment of my career," Lynne Tracy, who represented the US in Moscow from 2023 to 2025, tells the BBC.
US, UK and European diplomats once played a crucial role in Russia, defusing tensions even at the worst moments of the Cold War.
But now, deemed "unfriendly" by President Vladimir Putin&#x27;s government, Western ambassadors are under constant surveillance, isolated and increasingly cut off from the country they were sent to understand, as the war in Ukraine grinds on through its fifth year.
The BBC has spoken to five diplomats who have served in Russia since it launched its full-scale invasion.
They compare current pressure levels to the Cold War era - when Western embassies were monitored through physical surveillance, listening devices hidden inside gifts , and "spy dust" - a near invisible chemical agent used to track officials.
Hundreds of Russian diplomats were expelled from countries in Europe and the US after the war began in 2022. Moscow responded in kind, declaring hundreds of Western diplomats persona non grata and leaving many embassies short-staffed.
Tracy&#x27;s 30-year career took her to countries like Pakistan, where she survived an armed attack in 2008. But she says that even under constant physical threat, her posting in Peshawar was, in a sense, easier than Russia.
"There, I still felt that there were things within my control. What made Moscow more difficult were the high levels of uncertainty."
The constant pressure from Russian counterintelligence was the primary source of such uncertainty, she recalls.
Image caption, Tracy pictured leaving Russia&#x27;s Foreign Ministry after being summoned by Moscow in 2023
Russian diplomats in the West also complain of such unfair treatment. But according to the diplomats who spoke to the BBC, the scope and tactics of Moscow&#x27;s intelligence services are far greater.
"Expect listening devices, expect cameras in every single room of your house," a former European diplomat says of the warning he received before leaving for Moscow.
Another ex-ambassador says they were forced to dismiss two Russian employees after Moscow&#x27;s domestic security service, the FSB, tried to recruit them, demanding that they report on everything going on at the diplomatic mission.
The pair told the embassy instead, who let them go - "otherwise, they could have faced some sort of criminal prosecution".
Meanwhile, Russians who would ordinarily build relationships with diplomatic staff - such as those working in academia and culture - are also being intimidated into avoiding contact with Western officials.
One former ambassador says his guests were once "tailed" by FSB agents right up to the doors of the embassy.
Inside the shadowy world of anti-Putin sabotage from within Russia
Europe targeted by spiralling campaign of sabotage - and Russia is the chief suspect
Just this June, guests invited to the British ambassador&#x27;s residence in Moscow for the King&#x27;s birthday celebrations said they had been harassed by pro-Kremlin activists and state-media journalists, and then questioned by the police.
One of the guests says he has since declined several invitations to events at Western embassies out of fear.
One former ambassador says "constant paranoia" has driven a number of officials to quit their jobs. Even after leaving Russia himself, he adds, he was looking over his shoulder for months.
&#x27;Generals prove what they can do in war. The same is true for diplomats&#x27;
In the immediate aftermath of the invasion, there were discussions across Europe about whether to recall ambassadors or stop working in Russia altogether, says former French ambassador Pierre LÃ©vy.
He remembers calling Paris immediately to say pulling their envoys would be a mistake, feeling that the worse relations became, the more important they would be.
The invasion quickly transformed their work, says another ex-European ambassador; the co-operation required for their usual efforts on bilateral relations, trade and joint projects was now impossible.
And for those who stayed in Russia, the war left them with a host of new priorities beyond supporting their own citizens and companies.
Firstly, countries viewed as unfriendly by Moscow still wanted at least some channel of communication with Russian officials to get an accurate picture of what was happening inside the country.
But they also had to protect their staff and keep their embassies functioning.
Image caption, Pierre LÃ©vy (second from the left) and Lynne Tracy (second from the right) pictured at the funeral service for opposition leader Alexei Navalny in March 2024
After Russia&#x27;s ban on hiring foreign nationals in 2021, the US embassy&#x27;s facilities team was left significantly understaffed. Ðven a minor plumbing issue like a leaking pipe could turn into a major problem, draining the time of diplomatic workers.
One diplomat recalls discussing staffing shortages with a Russian official, who advised him not to get too upset. "He said, &#x27;at least you still have the chef&#x27;."
Financial sanctions imposed by Western governments to support Ukraine created different day-to-day difficulties. After Russian banks were cut off from the Swift payment network, and Mastercard and Visa suspended operations in Russia, many embassies struggled with basic operating expenses.
A former ambassador recalls simply running out of cash and being unable to pay his staff - until another diplomat, who was selling his car after being expelled, brought the rubles to the embassy in a suitcase.
"It&#x27;s completely insane," the diplomat laughs. "But you had to look at it with a sense of humour."
Image caption, A wooden replica of the Great Seal of the US was given to the American ambassador to Russia as a gift in 1945 - it was later found to contain a listening device
Russian officials make little secret of their attitude towards Western diplomats. They have openly accused those from "unfriendly countries" of engaging in "espionage".
Moscow has been particularly critical of those who show an interest in expressions of dissent against the Kremlin and, according to Russian journalist Alexei Venediktov, has effectively banned most Russian officials from speaking to Western embassies.
This has only worsened relations with the West, says Venediktov, who recalls telling Russian officials: "You are being misunderstood precisely because of your own actions - because ambassadors cannot do their jobs."
Today, Moscow has little to no contact with many Western governments, says one ex-European ambassador. "There are no calls, no meetings at ministerial level."
But they say this has made diplomats all the more important, left with the responsibility to communicate their governments&#x27; positions to Moscow.
"Generals prove what they can do during a war. The same is true for diplomats. When things get really difficult, every single thing you do actually matters."
Lynne Tracy left Moscow in 2025 feeling she had accomplished her primary mission: the US Embassy had held firm.
But she is also proud of the work she and her team did to maintain connection between Americans and ordinary Russians.
"Because at some point, government-to-government relations will have a chance to improve - and those ties between people will be there to help speed the improvement."
Russians turn to cash, putting more strain on slowing wartime economy
Threats, insults, and Kremlin &#x27;robots&#x27;: How Russian diplomacy died
Missiles hit Kyiv after Russia agrees to US call to resume Ukraine peace talks

</details>

Share Iran says seven ships hit in Hormuz Strait, warns US of wider response on social media
Crude oil tankers, bulk carriers and vessels sit anchored near Qaboos Port in Muscat, Oman [File: Elke Scholiers / Getty]
Iran says that seven oil tankers have been struck and sustained damage in the Strait of Hormuz, the largest single-day toll for the waterway since the United States and Israel started their war against Iran on February 28.
Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) said in a statement on Thursday that four of the vessels were transferring oil when they were targeted and caught fire overnight. The other three were disabled after striking mines.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 ‘You have to sacrifice’: Trump voters stay loyal as US midterms approach
list 2 of 3 Netanyahu promises Iran regime change: Can Israel topple Tehran government?
list 3 of 3 Rising costs and tight budgets strain Americans ahead of midterm elections
The claims could not be independently verified, and the report did not provide details about possible casualties or the tankers’ flags.
The IRGC also warned that it could strike US bases across the Middle East if Washington moves to help tankers transit other parts of the strait.
“The US military must know that if this trend continues, the conflict will extend to its bases in the region,” the IRGC said in a statement on its Sepah News website. “This is a serious warning.”
Iran claims control over Hormuz, a strategic shipping route through which one-fifth of the world’s oil supplies passed before war broke out. It regularly attacks ships that attempt to navigate the strait without its authorisation.
But the US military has been escorting vessels through the waterway in increasing numbers, with Iran responding by stepping up attacks.
US President Donald Trump said on Thursday that “Iran is doing everything they can to drive up the price of oil prior to the US Midterm Election”.
In a post on his Truth Social platform, he added: “They are making up fake hits, mines, and everything else because more oil is coming through the Hormuz Strait now than it did even prior to the beginning of the conflict.”
A US Central Command spokesperson, speaking anonymously to Al Jazeera, also called Iran’s mine claims incorrect.
The spokesperson said US forces have cleared the international shipping lanes of mines and that Iran is using missiles and drones.
The strait remains open, the spokesperson said, with 95 percent of vessels getting through.
The attacks, the spokesperson added, show Tehran’s “desperation” under the US blockade, which cuts Iran off from the resources it needs to rebuild the military capabilities the US has said it destroyed.
“The number of ships and tankers attacked every single day is increasing, and Iran is saying it is continuing to do this because they are not going to allow any change in that situation,” said Al Jazeera’s Mohamed Vall, reporting from Tehran.
“They have full control over the Strait of Hormuz right now, they say.”
Vall said there is also talk in Tehran about an imminent escalation in the war, with the Iranians expecting the US to attack even before US midterm elections in early November.
“The IRGC army chief has boasted that Iran is more ready than anytime before, and has produced enough weapons and equipment to wage a symmetrical war against the United States,” he said.
Reporting from the US White House, Al Jazeera’s Kimberly Halkett said: “Striking Iran does not appear to serve any Republican interests”, adding that pre-election polls tell the same story for Trump’s political party.
“What we’ve seen in surveys in recent months, and as of today, is that most Americans want relief from their tax money going to pay for any potential war with Iran that is ramped up,” Halkett said.
“There is real frustration over the rising cost for everyday Americans, given the rising fuel prices that have occurred as a result of this conflict  … As a result, the US president is having to make the case.
“The pressure is on the president, and it’s not just from the opposition party,” she said. “It is also from within the Republican Party as well.”

</details>

### 9. ★★★☆☆ Bond traders are backing off Fed rate hike bets — here's what it means for mortgage rates - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 💰 全球金融
- **来源**: GN Fed
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-22T18:05:07+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Bond traders are backing off Fed rate hike bets — here's what it means for mortgage rates&nbsp;&nbsp;Yaho

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Bond traders are backing off Fed rate hike bets — here's what it means for mortgage rates
If oil is about to spark an inflation wave, the bond market hasn't heard about it.
Brent crude ( BZ=F ) is trading around $112 a barrel, more than 50% higher than before the Iran war began in late February. Normally, that kind of spike would have bond investors worried about inflation and pushing interest rates higher.
Instead, Treasurys are rallying on Thursday as stocks slide. The 10-year Treasury yield ( ^TNX ) is on track for its biggest one-day drop in more than two years, slipping back below 5%. When bond prices rise, yields fall. That matters for anyone shopping for a home, because mortgage rates tend to follow the 10-year .
Behind the move is a quick change of heart about the Federal Reserve as long-term interest rates decline. The Fed meets next week, on Oct. 28, and investors have been bracing for it to raise rates to fight perceived oil-driven inflation.
Those bets are fading fast. Fed funds futures now put the odds of an October hike at about 5%, according to Bloomberg data. That's down from 15% on Wednesday and 37% at the end of September.
The outlook for December has shifted too. As recently as Wednesday, traders were fully priced for a hike by year-end. Now they see roughly a 75% chance.
Bloomberg, Yahoo Finance Why bond traders aren't panicking about oil
Even with oil soaring, the bond market's inflation forecast has barely budged.
One way to measure what investors expect is the gap between regular Treasurys and Treasury Inflation-Protected Securities, or TIPS , which pay extra when prices rise. That gap, called the breakeven rate , says investors expect inflation to average about 2.4% a year over the next five years. That's the same as before the war started.
So why did yields climb so much this year? Almost all of it came from "real" yields, which is what bonds pay after inflation. That's a bet the Fed will keep rates high enough to stop oil from turning into lasting inflation, not a bet that inflation is about to take off.
Bond yields soared on real rates, not inflation bets · Federal Reserve, Yahoo Finance Not everyone buys the market's calm. Bloomberg strategist Simon White argues breakevens are "mispricing" the oil shock.
The average 30-year fixed mortgage rate fell to 7.32% this week from 7.40%, according to Freddie Mac. That's the first drop in two months, after a seven-week climb that took rates to their highest level since 2023. Before the war, rates were just under 6%.
Freddie Mac's survey doesn't capture Thursday's bond rally. If yields stay lower, mortgage rates could follow in the coming days.
The damage from this fall's surge is already showing up. Pending home sales, which track signed contracts, fell to a record low in September , according to the National Association of Realtors. "Market conditions weakened in September due to fast-rising mortgage rates," said NAR chief economist Lawrence Yun.
Buyers who did sign are walking away. About 14.5% of homes fell out of contract in September, according to Redfin data. That's the highest share on record outside the early months of the pandemic.
The bond rally held up through Thursday afternoon's sale of five-year TIPS. Bloomberg strategist Cameron Crise saw a sign that some big investors were scrambling to unwind bets on higher rates.
The next big test is the Fed decision next Wednesday.

### 11. ★★★☆☆ If a Stock Market Crash Is on the Way, Warren Buffett Says This Is the Best Thing Investors Can Do for Their Portfolio - The Motley Fool

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T10:37:11+00:00
- **发生了什么**: If a Stock Market Crash Is on the Way, Warren Buffett Says This Is the Best Thing Investors Can Do for Their Portfolio&nbsp;&nbsp;The Motley Fool

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The S&P 500 ( ^GSPC -1.21% ) and Nasdaq Composite ( ^IXIC -2.60% ) have recently been experiencing a bout of volatility after repeatedly notching record highs throughout 2026. With stocks coming under pressure, some investors are wondering whether a larger market correction is approaching.
Part of the concern stems from elevated bond yields and stubborn inflation. Meanwhile, artificial intelligence (AI) hyperscalers like Amazon , Microsoft , Alphabet , and Meta Platforms are among the companies supporting the broader economy as they accelerate spending on data centers .
Whether this volatility is just a temporary pullback or the beginning of something worse is impossible to know. But legendary investor Warren Buffett spent decades explaining how to navigate markets dominated by exciting new technologies, sky-high valuations, and uncertainty.
Former Berkshire Hathaway CEO Warren Buffett. Image source: Getty Images.
Revolutionary technology does not equal a good investment
Buffett built Berkshire Hathaway  into one of history&#x27;s most successful investment firms simply because he had a relentless focus on businesses he understands. He generally avoided technology stocks, instead favoring industries such as insurance, banking, consumer products, utilities, and energy. These businesses usually have relatively predictable economics, which allowed Buffett to better assess their long-term earnings potential.
Buffett understood that an industry can revolutionize society without producing attractive returns overall. Consider the automobile and aviation industries as examples. Cars and airplanes have unquestionably transformed the global economy, but countless early manufacturers and airlines have gone bankrupt as competition intensified.
The theme here is that identifying which industries are game changers is much easier than choosing the specific companies that will capture profits in the long run. This distinction matters in today&#x27;s AI environment.
While AI is shaping up to be one of the most transformative technologies in history, that doesn&#x27;t mean every semiconductor company, software developer, or infrastructure provider is going to generate market-beating returns.
Is the AI revolution destined to end up like the dot-com bubble?
During the late 1990s, investors were pouring money into virtually anything that resembled the internet. Speculative technology stocks became multibaggers , even when the underlying companies had little revenue, no profits, or incomplete business models.
Reality inevitably caught up with expectations. After peaking in March 2000, the Nasdaq plummeted by 78% by October 2002. Suddenly, once-promising internet businesses left investors holding the bag.
Today, some investors are worried the AI boom could become a modern retelling of the dot-com bubble. Sure, AI leaders like Nvidia and the cloud hyperscalers already generate substantial profits and cash flow. The reason why is that their AI infrastructure is supported by numerous established businesses rather than simply promises around future growth. Still, the industry faces uncertainty because not every model developer is going to succeed in the long run.
This brings us to Buffett&#x27;s most important investing principle. The single best thing an investor can do for their portfolio right now is load up on companies with durable competitive advantages rather than chasing the stocks attracting the most attention. Buffett describes these advantages as economic moats that come in the form of powerful brands, high switching costs, network effects , and economies of scale.
There&#x27;s a more subtle lesson investors can learn from the dot-com crash: Time in the market is more valuable than trying to time the market. Suppose you invested in the S&P 500 near its peak during the dot-com era. If you managed to stay invested, you would have witnessed the index appreciate roughly 450%.
A hypothetical $10,000 investment would have grown to nearly $55,000, even though it was made right before a major market collapse. What&#x27;s even more inspiring is that over the last 25 years, the S&P 500 has endured three recessions -- pictured above as the grey-shaded columns -- and managed to come out higher after each one. This underscores the resiliency of the S&P 500 as one of the greatest wealth-creation vehicles.
This performance is why Buffett&#x27;s emphasis on diversification is both valuable and relevant in today&#x27;s market. Rather than panic-selling whenever stocks become volatile or valuation is frothy, investors should rebalance their portfolios if their positions expose them to unnecessary risk.
Ultimately, no one knows whether the recent sell-off will permeate into a full-blown crash. But Buffett&#x27;s philosophy offers a practical response: The best opportunity is to own quality businesses, avoid paying unreasonable prices, and remain patient enough to let compounding do the heavy lifting.

### 13. ★★★☆☆ Are global oil reserves sufficient to withstand renewed US-Iran war? - Al Jazeera

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics / GN Energy
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T07:42:08+00:00
- **发生了什么**: <a href="https://news.google.com/rss/articles/CBMirgFBVV95cUxPanhMUG9fMjBOTDd6UGhPUnUzd1pPcTVseGNYeEY0SXZPZXpEZVlkREFUVFRUQkgwSEpXQkgwNVF4T29pdGlkWTFpYWhzamt4U1I3NVhUVmxGM3hnUzRCMkdPZm1zb3FjeWRYYXJLcTdaTFNNQTRwS21hbjlibzhoQWt3VG1oaU5tQ2tLRV9IazZqTVFabzBINmNKZ1BSY1lxN0NqVFVFU2ZJYktuTGfSAbMBQVVfeXFMUG1ncnBSWUd5S0F0LUx5c2RSVjk4QlFPbGlpUWEwZkhKSVRoYVhiU2FmS2lLWm5mdTR4LU8xMEJlRF9fU3Nua2psYmM3T1ROdDN4VUltMVc1YUc0OUFOMzI3UzB3ZGdWRHFiX0dEdlpiai1aOWR5eFoyaV9qbEVHQUZ6X3hMelBRVkg2STlzNm0zQ0wxTjhvb1p0R1o5cl

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Are global oil reserves sufficient to withstand renewed US-Iran war? on social media
Vessels are seen anchored off Bandar Abbas, Iran, along the Strait of Hormuz [File: Amirhossein Khorgooei/ISNA via AFP]
Oil prices have climbed towards their highest levels in five months as attacks on tankers in and around the Strait of Hormuz mount and efforts to end the United States-Israel war on Iran are deadlocked.
Brent crude, the international benchmark, rose by more than $2 to $110.85 a barrel on Wednesday, close to a five-month peak.
list of 4 items list 1 of 4 US-sanctioned ICC wins Sakharov Prize, EU’s top human rights award
list 2 of 4 Trump says Musk restored Ukraine’s Starlink access amid Russia war
list 3 of 4 Will ICE agents play a role in the 2026 US midterm elections?
list 4 of 4 Ilhan Omar plans to move out of her Washington DC home after federal raid
A day earlier, Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) said two tankers were “set ablaze” after hitting mines at what it called an illegal crossing point in the strait. Then, on Thursday, the IRGC said it hit seven more tankers although Washington dismissed the claim. In a statement, the IRGC said four of the vessels were transferring oil when they were targeted and caught fire overnight.
In September, the Houthis, an armed group also known as Ansar Allah (Arabic for Supporters of God), seized territory in Yemen close to the Bab al-Mandeb strait, a critical chokepoint into and out of the Red Sea and a gateway to Egypt’s Suez Canal.
The escalation came as the world’s emergency oil stocks, already tapped heavily since the Iran war began on February 28, sit at their lowest levels in decades.
Al Jazeera put a question to analysts: If fighting resumes in full, the Strait of Hormuz is shut off completely and Houthi attacks choke off Bab al-Mandeb, how long could the world’s reserves last?
Strategic oil reserves are stockpiles of crude oil and fuel held by governments or by companies on their behalf for use in a supply emergency.
The International Energy Agency (IEA), which was established in 1974 after the Arab oil embargo, coordinates most of them. Its importing members must hold at least 90 days of net imports. China and India are not members.
How much of the reserves has already been released this year?
On March 11, days after the start of the US-Israel war on Iran, the IEA’s 32 members agreed to make 400 million barrels available from emergency supplies, more than double the volume released after Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in 2022.
The US pledged 172.2 million barrels from its Strategic Petroleum Reserve (SPR) and Japan 79.8 million, according to IEA figures.
The SPR has since fallen to 283 million barrels, its lowest level since October 1982, according to the US Department of Energy.
Much of that oil was lent rather than sold and must be returned with extra barrels, according to the US Energy Information Administration.
Far less than before the war began in February, and not all of it can be used.
Aaron Kildow, a senior crude oil analyst at Sparta Commodities, told Al Jazeera that global strategic stocks excluding China and Iran have fallen by nearly 327 million barrels since the end of December 2025 with the US, Europe and Japan accounting for about 95 percent of the draw.
He estimated that leaves only about 400 million barrels of usable reserves worldwide, excluding China’s stockpile, which is unlikely to be exported, and Gulf stocks, which could be cut off by a total closure of Hormuz.
Nearly half of these available stocks are in Japan.
Europe has about 76 million barrels uncommitted, but members such as Germany are holding some back for a local emergency, Kildow said.
“Europe is the real wildcard, both on timing and willingness,” Kildow said.
Meanwhile, China’s onshore crude stocks stand at 1.13 billion barrels, down from 1.23 billion in late February but still enough for about 80 days of refinery intake, Muyu Xu, a senior crude oil analyst at the analytics firm Kpler, told Al Jazeera.
India’s reserves cover just eight days of its imports, the Reuters news agency reported.
What would a full closure of Hormuz and Bab al-Mandeb mean?
Before the war, about one-fifth of the world’s oil and liquefied natural gas supplies, or roughly 20 million barrels of oil a day, passed through the Strait of Hormuz.
Saudi Arabia has increased the amount of crude it transports from its oilfields in the east to its Red Sea port of Yanbu in the west via the East-West Pipeline, thereby bypassing Hormuz.
But there have been strikes on that pipeline, and tankers heading south from Yanbu must pass through Bab a-Mandeb, where the Iran-backed Houthis declared a blockade on Saudi shipping in July.
Only a confirmed 373,000 barrels a day of Saudi crude have transited Bab al-Mandeb so far this month, the lowest level since February, Xu said, with most Yanbu cargoes now heading north via Egypt instead.
If Hormuz were shut and the Houthis blocked all Saudi tankers, Gulf producers could export only through a few pipelines and Yanbu’s northbound route, Xu said.
That would leave about 7.5 million barrels a day of Middle Eastern crude, against 16.8 million now, a shortfall of about 9.3 million barrels a day, she estimated.
“If such attacks occur, the supply disruption could be even more severe and prolonged,” Xu said, referring to strikes on oilfields, terminals and pipelines.
Not for long – possibly just weeks, according to experts.
If Kpler’s estimated shortfall had to be covered entirely from the roughly 400 million usable barrels that Kildow identified, they could not, in practice, be released that fast. Japanese and South Korean reserves can move quickly, Kildow said, but US barrels are staged over three months, and Europe’s contribution is uncertain.
Chris Beauchamp, chief market analyst at IG Group, said the world was fortunate after the first closure. “The salient feature of March and beyond was how a crisis didn’t actually develop. We got away with it,” he said.
“I don’t think the global economy will be as lucky next time if there is a protracted conflict,” Beauchamp said. “A month or two is all we have.”
The world would not literally run out of oil. Instead, prices would rise until enough demand disappeared. “One hundred fifty dollars [per barrel] becomes a very distinct possibility, which then sees significant demand destruction,” Beauchamp said.
The sharpest squeeze would be on diesel, which is already nearly double the price of crude, the IEA said.
Refiners would compete for crude grades rich in diesel, “a cost that ultimately lands on consumers,” Kildow said.
Most Gulf exports in September and October went to Asian buyers, and India and Pakistan are among the most exposed, Kildow said, adding that their nearest alternative supply, from West Africa, is 25 to 28 days away by sea, compared with two days from Fujairah in the United Arab Emirates.
“The squeeze won’t hit uniformly. It lands fastest and hardest where reserves are thinnest and substitute supply is farthest away,” Kildow said.
In the second quarter of this year, as the US-Iran war was under way, ExxonMobil reported earnings of $14.5bn, its highest in four years while Chevron posted $12bn, its best in six years and Shell more than doubled its earnings to nearly $10bn. Their profits are largely down to the high oil prices.
A second shock would send refiners scrambling for supplies from the US, Latin America, West Africa and Russia, Xu said.
“This would allow non-Middle Eastern producers to benefit from higher crude prices, potentially alongside lower transportation costs,” she said.
In August, US President Donald Trump said companies such as ExxonMobil and Chevron were “making too much money based on a shortage”.

U.S. oil holds above $100, Brent at five-month high
A drone view shows a fuel vessel docked at the Baniyas oil terminal in Baniyas, Syria, Oct. 12, 2026.
Crude oil futures ticked higher on Friday, with global benchmark Brent crude hovering at a five-month high.
By 6:51 a.m. ET, front-month Brent crude oil futures were 1.1% higher at $114.49 per barrel. U.S. West Texas Intermediate crude futures were up 1.6% to trade at $101.04 a barrel.
Brent futures were on track for a weekly rise of 3.3%, putting the contracts on course for their third consecutive week of gains. They were also trading at their highest since early May.
WTI futures were on track for a weekly decline of 1.1%, putting the contracts on course to snap two consecutive weeks of gains.
Traders are continuing to monitor developments in the Middle East.
On Thursday, U.S. President Donald Trump accused Iran of lying about attacks on ships transiting the Strait of Hormuz.
"Iran is doing everything they can to drive up the price of Oil prior to the U.S. Midterm Election," he wrote on Truth Social. "They are even making up fake hits, mines, and everything else, because more Oil is coming through the Hormuz Strait now than it did even prior to the beginning of the Conflict."
Iran&#x27;s Islamic Revolution Guards Corps said Thursday that four vessels had been struck and caught fire in the Persian Gulf, according to the Iranian state-affiliated Tasnim news agency, while a further three had struck mines in the Strait of Hormuz.
Separately, Iran&#x27;s army chief was reported to have said the country&#x27;s military is fully prepared for "asymmetric warfare" after strengthening its capabilities.
In Saudi Arabia, oil giant Saudi Aramco reportedly told staff to evacuate its Dhahran headquarters and work remotely following a series of attacks by Iran-backed Houthi rebels, Reuters reported .
The Houthis have launched strikes on Saudi airports and some Aramco offices in recent weeks, resulting in multiple fatalities.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Trump says Musk restored Ukraine’s Starlink access amid Russia war on social media
Ukrainian officials have reported slow connectivity with Starlink, a key tool for their defence systems [Odd Andersen/AFP]
By Al Jazeera Staff , Reuters and The Associated Press
United States President Donald Trump has said that tech mogul Elon Musk has restored Ukraine’s access to Starlink, a satellite internet service that plays a key role in Kyiv’s war effort and air defences.
On Thursday, Trump declined to discuss a report from The Wall Street Journal that he had called Musk and instructed him to end restrictions on Ukraine’s connection.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 What is Starshield, and who limited Ukraine’s access to it?
list 2 of 3 ‘I don’t care how it’s done’: Trump pressures Ukraine to cut peace deal
list 3 of 3 Putin and Zelenskyy back three-way US peace talks on Ukraine
The news outlet reported that Starlink services returned to normal following the call.
“I don’t want to comment on it, whether I called or whether somebody else called, doesn’t make any difference, I guess. The bottom line, he put it back,” Trump told reporters when asked about the report.
On October 13, Ukrainian officials announced that sudden restrictions on Starlink services had left the country’s military scrambling.
Though the system came back online, connectivity has reportedly remained spotty.
Questions have persisted about where the decision to restrict access came from, with Musk and the Trump administration officials trading blame.
Trump has denied ordering restrictions on Ukraine’s access to Starlink.
“Well, I don’t know if it was anybody, but it wasn’t me, and I don’t think it was Elon,” he said on Friday. “And from what I understand, it’s the same as it’s always been.”
US Secretary of State Marco Rubio also said this week that decisions about Starlink’s speed and access belong to Musk.
“The bottom line is that that is governed by a private commercial contract as to how ⁠their stuff can be used and what speeds they’ll allow it to be used at,” Rubio said on Wednesday.
But Musk has denied any responsibility , stating that Starshield, the military-grade version of Starlink, is “owned and controlled by the US government”.
“Any limits on speed or usage are decided by the US military and ultimately POTUS,” he said in a social media post on October 14, using the acronym for “president of the United States”.
The Starlink speed caps and restrictions have also raised concerns that Trump, or the president’s right-wing allies, might seek to leverage such technology to pressure Ukraine into complying with US demands.
Trump has had a contentious relationship with Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy, repeatedly calling him “difficult” in comparison to his Russian counterpart, Vladimir Putin.
Ukraine has been locked in a slow-grinding war with Russia since February 2022, after Putin launched a full-scale invasion of the Eastern European country.
While Europe has backed Ukraine’s defence against the invasion, Trump has suggested Ukraine itself could be to blame for the conflict.
In 2025, for instance, Trump blamed Ukraine’s ambitions of joining NATO. “ I actually think that that was the thing that caused the start of the war,” he said at the time.
More recently, Trump posted on Truth Social that it was Zelenskyy’s fault the war had not yet ended. “He doesn’t want to end this war, and probably never did,” Trump wrote.
As the war continues, Trump has been criticised by Democrats and some members of his own Republican Party for easing US pressure on Russia.
Recently, his administration issued a series of announcements to buy Russian diesel and ease restrictions on Russia’s energy sector.
Those decisions come as Trump is struggling with the political fallout of high fuel prices caused by his decision to join Israel in launching a war on Iran.
He has also suggested he would be open to a trilateral meeting with Putin and Chinese President Xi Jinping, which would be a first for the three leaders.

- **发生了什么**: Brent crude rises to $114.72 a barrel after Tehran threatens retaliatory attacks on Gulf oil and tech facilitiesBusiness live – latest updatesOil has hit its highest level in more than five months as fears of fresh escalation in the Middle East conflict grip the energy markets.Brent crude, the internatio

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Tensions in the region have been rising this week, with Iran claiming to have struck commercial ships in the strait of Hormuz, and Washington ordering some US diplomats to leave Saudi Arabia. Photograph: Amr Alfiky/Reuters
Tensions in the region have been rising this week, with Iran claiming to have struck commercial ships in the strait of Hormuz, and Washington ordering some US diplomats to leave Saudi Arabia. Photograph: Amr Alfiky/Reuters
Oil hits five-month high amid fears US will renew strikes on Iran
Brent crude rises to $114.72 a barrel after Tehran threatens retaliatory attacks on Gulf oil and tech facilities
Oil has hit its highest level in more than five months as fears of fresh escalation in the Middle East conflict grip the energy markets.
Brent crude, the international benchmark for oil, rose by 1% on Friday morning to $114.72 a barrel – its highest level since May.
The increase came after Tehran said it would strike oil facilities, undersea cables, datacentres and AI facilities in the Gulf region if a new US-Israeli attack was launched against Iran .
“Widespread reports that the US is considering renewed military strikes on Iran after the midterm elections rather than pursuing a diplomatic route was enough for the price to pop, after what many had considered would be a brief conflict is now in its eighth month,” said Richard Hunter at the broker Interactive Investor.
Oil had traded at about $72 a barrel back in February, before the Iran war began, but soared to $126 a barrel by the end of April.
Tensions in the region have been rising this week; Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps claimed to have struck four commercial ships in the strait of Hormuz on Wednesday. The US state department ordered some diplomats and the families of US government employees to leave Saudi Arabia , which has faced continued attacks from Houthi rebels in Yemen.
Susannah Streeter, the chief investment strategists at the broker Wealth Club, said fears were rising that American and Israeli forces would be ordered to launch strikes on Iran’s energy infrastructure after the US elections on 3 November.
“At the same time, the attacks on Saudi Arabia’s facilities by Houthi rebels have intensified this week, and for ships attempting to cross the strait of Hormuz, it’s still a dangerous journey, with projectiles still being fired,” she said.
Streeter said Ukraine’s bombardment of Russian diesel refineries was also adding to supply concerns.

### 34. ★★★☆☆ Chip Stocks Fall as Supply Warning Deepens AI Selloff: Marvell and AMD Sink 8%, Intel Drops 7% - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 🤖 AI/科技
- **来源**: GN AI
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-22T15:45:08+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Chip Stocks Fall as Supply Warning Deepens AI Selloff: Marvell and AMD Sink 8%, Intel Drops 7%&nbsp;&nbsp;Yahoo Finance

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Chip Stocks Fall as Supply Warning Deepens AI Selloff: Marvell and AMD Sink 8%, Intel Drops 7%
Marvell and AMD each plunge 8% while Intel falls 7%, leading a chip selloff concentrated among stocks tied to AI data center spending.
Lam Research's CEO warned supply constraints could limit chip output through 2027, dragging the SOXX semiconductor ETF down 6%.
Lam's CEO called current tightness nothing like COVID shortages and expects the industry to catch up very shortly.
A chipmaking equipment maker's supply warning is turning the artificial intelligence (AI) trade into a broad semiconductor selloff, and the chip designers most tied to data center spending are taking the heaviest hits. Marvell Technology ( NASDAQ:MRVL ) stock is down 8% to $224.64 in morning trading, a drop that puts the custom chip designer at the front of the sector's retreat. Likewise, Advanced Micro Devices ( NASDAQ:AMD ) stock is down 8% to $509.79, matching Marvell's slide as one of the largest AI chip names gives back ground.
MF3d / Getty Images Across the group, the iShares Semiconductor ETF ( NASDAQ:SOXX ) is down 6%, a slide deep enough to show the damage spreading well past a handful of names. Intel ( NASDAQ:INTC ) stock is down 7% to $86.64, trailing Marvell and AMD while still falling faster than that sector fund.
Large-cap tech is holding up better, with the Invesco QQQ Trust ( NASDAQ:QQQ ) down 2%. That gap focuses the selling in chips and leaves the rest of the technology complex relatively steady, even after a rough stretch for the AI trade. Chip names are taking the brunt of a single comment from an equipment supplier.

The trigger came from Lam Research ( NASDAQ:LRCX ), which makes the equipment chipmakers use to build semiconductors. On its October 21 earnings call, Lam's chief financial officer, Doug Bettinger, stated that "this revenue guide would have been a bit higher if not for some near-term supply chain constraints that we are actively managing." Investors read that December-quarter comment as a sign that supply limits could hold back chip sales across the industry.
Tim Archer, Lam's chief executive, pointed to a longer runway for the bottleneck, saying: "As we move into calendar year 2027, we see clean room space availability and supply chain constraints for certain components continuing to limit chip makers and equipment companies' ability to fully meet end demand." Lam's management also stated that "The whole industry is going to have to work through very similar things," a line that extends the warning well beyond equipment makers. This latest wave of selling comes after a run of big technology earnings reports that put the AI trade under pressure.
Marvell and AMD Lead Losses as Selling Reaches Intel
AMD designs processors and graphics hardware for data centers and PCs, while Marvell develops custom chips and networking silicon for cloud providers. Among the chip designers most tied to data center AI spending, AMD and Marvell are also the stocks falling hardest. Marvell relies on manufacturing partners for fabrication, assembly, testing, and packaging, which links its results closely to outside capacity.
Intel both designs chips and manufactures them in its own factories. Having fabs hasn't spared Intel stock, which is still falling faster than the semiconductor fund, a sign the selling reaches chipmakers whatever their business model. Heavier spending on equipment, clean room space, and substrates is part of Intel's growth plan, and those inputs overlap with the bottlenecks Lam described.
Lam's own commentary offers a balance to the gloom around Marvell, AMD and Intel shares. Archer stated that "this is nothing like that" when comparing the current tightness with COVID-era shortages, adding, "I think we'll be caught up very shortly." Strong demand is what's straining suppliers in the first place, and Lam said some of its vendors are seeing orders from it grow even faster than Lam itself.
For Marvell, AMD and Intel, the question now is whether supply limits start showing up in their own outlooks. Bettinger expects shipments that slip out of the December quarter to go out in the March quarter, giving the market a concrete timeline for the bottleneck. Marvell's next outlook could show whether its data center momentum holds against tighter capacity.
For now, the semiconductor fund's steeper drop against large-cap tech keeps the pressure squarely on chipmakers. Investors should consider keeping their position sizes modest until the chip supply squeeze resolves.

U.S. stock futures are leaning slightly lower early Friday as Wall Street navigates geopolitical stalemates, soaring oil prices, and fresh regulatory scrutiny on prediction markets.
The Polymarket crowd, a Polygon (CRYPTO: POL) based prediction platform, was nearly evenly split for the Oct. 23 trading session. The "S&P 500 (SPX) Up or Down on October 23?" contract currently reflects a 49% chance of a higher open.
Traders are balancing a subdued futures market against sticky inflation drivers and significant corporate earnings:
Slightly Negative Index Futures: Equity futures are pointing to a moderately red open. Dow Jones futures dropped 0.30%. S&P 500 futures fell 0.14%, Nasdaq 100 futures slipped 0.03%, and Russell 2000 futures declined 0.23%.
Geopolitics & Surging Oil: Iran told Pakistan's army chief that it will refuse talks with Washington until its demands—including the lifting of the U.S. blockade—are met, reiterating that all enriched nuclear materials will remain in Iran, Reuters reported. Meanwhile, CFTC Enforcement Director David Miller confirmed the agency is probing suspicious oil trading activity surrounding the U.S.-Iran conflict. Energy prices continued to surge amid the gridlock; Brent climbed 0.83% to $114.11 a barrel, and WTI crude crossed the century mark, gaining 1.13% to $100.59 a barrel.
Earnings & Eco Data: Friday's economic docket includes October's S&P flash U.S. manufacturing and services PMI at 9:45 a.m. ET, followed by the final University of Michigan consumer survey at 10:00 a.m. ET. The earnings calendar features American Express Co. (NYSE: AXP ), SLB NV (NYSE: SLB ), and more.
While geopolitical tensions and high energy costs weigh heavily on traditional equities, the AI sector remains a massive focal point for valuations. Valuation expert Aswath Damodaran highlighted the upcoming Anthropic IPO, noting that the AI firm could price between $1.5 trillion and $2 trillion.
Damodaran cautioned that young AI companies are currently being priced on unconventional metrics like annualized revenue run rates (ARR)—similar to how dot-com era stocks were priced on "eyeballs"—making them incredibly difficult to evaluate through traditional intrinsic valuation methods.
The Oct. 22 Polymarket contract resolved "Down". The contract recorded $25,773 in total trading volume
On Thursday, the SPDR S&P 500 ETF Trust (NYSE: SPY ) and Invesco QQQ Trust ETF (NASDAQ: QQQ ), which track the S&P 500 and Nasdaq-100, respectively, closed lower. SPY fell 1.18% to $763.96, while QQQ declined 2.86% to $704.47. Meanwhile, the Dow tracker, State Street SPDR Dow Jones Industrial Average ETF Trust (NYSE: DIA ), ended 0.04% higher at $527.89.

### 38. ★★★☆☆ Five Israelis wounded and two suspected Palestinian attackers killed in West Bank shooting

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / GN Top / NPR World
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T10:26:06+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The attack comes at a time of heightened tensions in the occupied West Bank and four days before Israel's general election.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Photos of one of the suspected attackers were shared by AFP news agency
Five Israelis have been wounded and two suspected Palestinian attackers killed by Israeli security forces following a shooting in the occupied West Bank, Israeli officials say.
Footage verified by the BBC shows an intense gun battle between soldiers and the gunmen, at a military checkpoint at the entrance to the city of Hebron.
In response, Israel&#x27;s Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu said he had ordered the military to prepare for a full lockdown of the West Bank, beginning later on Friday.
The attack comes at a time of high tensions, with extremist Jewish settlers targeting Palestinians during the annual olive harvest, and Israel&#x27;s general election taking place in four days time.
The two injured Israelis are in critical condition, according to Israel&#x27;s ambulance service.
The Israeli military said it had found a large quantity of ammunition in the attackers&#x27; vehicle, and that they believe the gunmen had intended to carry out a much larger attack on the nearby Route 35, but ran into the checkpoint - so instead decided to open fire there.
Unverified CCTV footage shows two gunmen exit a car armed with what appears to be a handgun and an assault rifle and start firing on soldiers at the checkpoint, before coming under return fire.
The Israeli military said it had begun raids on the homes of the attackers, while far-right Finance Minister Bezalel Smotrich said he had ordered the demolition of several Palestinian buildings adjacent to the site of the attack.
It is common practice for Israeli forces to demolish the homes of Palestinians who are suspected of carrying out attacks on Israelis.
Netanyahu, who has built his election campaign on promising security for Israelis, said that his Likud party would cancel campaign events for the morning.
The West Bank was already expected to be placed under lockdown for the election on Monday and Tuesday, according to Israeli media.
At least 96 Palestinians have been killed by Israeli forces or settlers in the West Bank since the start of the year, according to the UN.
Four Israelis, both security forces and civilians, have been killed by Palestinians there over the same period, the UN says.
Israeli police arrest 10 over settler attacks on journalist and diplomats in West Bank

### 39. ★★★☆☆ US-Iran war, then and now: How the landscape has changed since February

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T11:43:53+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The positions of Washington and Tehran have changed dramatically since US-Israeli strikes began the war, analysts say.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share US-Iran war, then and now: How the landscape has changed since February on social media
Pro-government mourners walk beneath a portrait of former Iranian Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei during a religious rally in Tehran on August 4, 2026 [Morteza Nikoubazl/NurPhoto via Getty Images]
Iran says seven oil tankers have caught fire or sustained damage in and around the Strait of Hormuz as its Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) warns that renewed US attacks could trigger a wider response and the world braces for the possibility of renewed hostilities .
Although analysts consider a US attack before the November 3 midterm elections unlikely, sabre-rattling on both sides has raised fears that escalating rhetoric could give way to renewed attacks with control of the Strait of Hormuz and Iranian nuclear facilities at the heart of the conflict.
list of 4 items list 1 of 4 Macron to meet student groups: Where are France’s protests heading?
list 2 of 4 Ultra-Orthodox conscription debate ignites Israel’s election battleground
list 3 of 4 Israeli military kills two in gun battle in occupied West Bank
list 4 of 4 Hamilton calls for Palestine and DR Congo freedom as Rwanda takes F1 spotlight
But the critical Gulf waterway, through which one-fifth of global oil and liquefied natural gas supplies were shipped before the war, was not even an issue before the United States and Israel first launched strikes on Iran on February 28. Furthermore, talks over Iran’s nuclear programme were under way at the time.
As the US wrestles with depleted defensive weapons stocks and a global energy crisis mounts, how does the land lie now before  potential renewed strikes and how does it differ from February before any of this began?
Unlike in February before the war began, the US and Iran would be fighting it out on several different fronts if attacks resume.
While the objective of US-Israeli strikes on Iran was initially to topple its leadership, another aim was to weaken Iran’s regional allies, including Hamas and Hezbollah. Announcing the offensive, Trump pledged to prevent Tehran-backed groups from attacking US forces and destabilising the region.
While the offensive failed to force a change in Iran’s government, Hamas has been severely weakened as Israel continues to attack Gaza – with more than 1,400 Palestinians killed since a US-backed “ceasefire” was announced in October last year.
Hezbollah’s retaliation against targets in northern Israel after the killing of Iranian Supreme Leader Ali Khamenei in the first US-Israeli air strikes brought Lebanon into the war in early March.
Israel launched a bombing campaign against sites in southern Lebanon and the suburbs of Beirut it considers Hezbollah strongholds and, at one point, occupied about one-fifth of the country, displacing more than one million people. It still occupies significant land on Lebanon’s southern border.
The biggest geographical change, however, has been the emergence of the Gulf’s waterways and energy infrastructure as key targets. Iranian attacks on US military assets and civilian infrastructure have exposed Gulf countries to the consequences of a war they had hoped to avoid.
Urban Coningham, a research fellow at the Royal United Services Institute, told Al Jazeera: “US-Iran negotiations had previously centred on Iran’s regional allies and nuclear programme. The Strait of Hormuz and attacks on the Gulf have now become central points of contention.”
While the Gulf has experienced attacks before, Coningham said the scale and “velocity” of recent Iranian strikes, including on oil and gas infrastructure, have marked a significant shift.
The Strait of Hormuz between Iran and Oman, which had been freely open to international shipping before the war, now gives Tehran a means of imposing costs far beyond its borders. Iran continues to threaten and attack shipping there while the US maintains its own blockade of Iranian ports. Traffic has been severely disrupted and supplies of oil and gas have been sharply reduced.
In March, CNN reported that the Pentagon and US National Security Council had significantly underestimated Iran’s willingness to restrict passage through the strait and the consequences of that. Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth rejected the reporting, insisting the military had planned for that possibility.
Renewed fighting between the forces of the Saudi-backed, internationally recognised government of Yemen and the Iran-backed Houthis has added another front to the regional crisis. Yemen’s war has expanded to Saudi Arabia as Houthi missile and drone attacks target Saudi airports and oil infrastructure.
This front has brought another trade chokepoint into sharper focus – Bab al-Mandeb, the strait that links the Red Sea to the Gulf of Aden. Houthi threats against Saudi-linked shipping there could compound transportation disruptions in Hormuz, undermine efforts to move Gulf exports through alternative routes and place additional pressure on the US.
The two countries’ leaders face different constraints. Iran’s rulers remain concerned about unrest and their own survival but do not face the same electoral reckoning as Trump’s Republican Party, which faces midterm elections in less than two weeks.
Before the February strikes, Iran’s government faced deep public anger after its deadly crackdown in January on nationwide protests against high inflation and the plummeting value of Iran’s currency. US-Israeli attacks subsequently killed Khamenei, who had ruled since 1989, and security chief Ali Larijani. But they did not topple the Islamic Republic.
Khamenei’s son Mojtaba succeeded him. His elevation and the influence of the IRGC has reinforced the position of hardliners within the government.
Coningham cautioned that the absence of demonstrations during a war should not be interpreted as support for the government.
“While the conflict had produced a rallying effect around resistance to foreign attacks, that did not mean Iran had emerged stronger,” he said.
Meanwhile, Trump faces much tougher political pressure before any renewed strikes. His approval rating has fallen from 40 percent in a Reuters/Ipsos poll reported days before the war began to just 31 percent in a survey released on Monday – the lowest recorded by the pollster across either of Trump’s presidential terms – amid frustration over high petrol prices and the deep unpopularity of the war on Iran among Americans.
Iran’s economy was already struggling before the war, weighed down by sanctions and assets frozen abroad. The conflict has deepened those pressures as the US combines military force, more sanctions and a naval blockade to squeeze the country further.
In April, Iran’s government estimated the direct and indirect damage from US-Israeli attacks at $270bn. The pressure intensified in late August when Washington launched Operation Economic Outcast , seeking to isolate Iran from international trade by imposing penalties on those doing business with it.
Official figures showed Iran’s gross domestic product contracted by 10.1 percent year-on-year from March 21 to June 20. Energy exports have been severely curtailed by the US blockade while the rial has plunged in value.
The US has also incurred substantial costs. In September, the Congressional Budget Office estimated that the conflict had cost the Department of Defense $38bn as of August 1. It said continued operations were expected to cost about $2bn to $3bn a month, depending on the intensity of fighting.
As a result of the additional costs of the war, US government debt surpassed a record $40 trillion in August – two years before it had been expected to reach that level.
While the US has demonstrated its ability to strike deep inside Iran, that destructive capacity has not brought down Iran’s political system nor has it finished off Tehran’s ability to retaliate with strikes on US assets and energy infrastructure in neighbouring countries.
Coningham said the difficulty for Washington is “translating military superiority into strategic results”. He also described recent troop redeployments as possible military posturing, but he added that the deployments do represent a shift from earlier efforts to reduce the US military footprint in parts of the region.
US media reports suggested there is now a severe shortage of weapons in the US arsenal in the Middle East as expensive defensive missiles have been depleted against a bombardment of cheap Iranian drones.
Fox News reported that the USS Theodore Roosevelt aircraft carrier and the 13th Marine Expeditionary Unit were expected to reach the Middle East by the end of November, bringing about 10,000 military personnel. The deployment would expand Trump’s military options as he weighs further action.
Coningham said, for Iran, the conflict fits a longstanding narrative of resistance against what its leadership portrayed as US and Israeli colonialism and imperialism.
Since February 28, the IRGC has demonstrated its skill at “asymmetric warfare” as it has held off expensive US military might with cheap, mass-produced drones.
Furthermore, its use of “decentralised structures” has allowed it to continue operating despite disrupted communications and the loss of senior decision-makers, Coningham said.
US strikes have severely damaged Iran’s navy and increased its reliance on smaller vessels, including speedboats used in recent attacks in the Strait of Hormuz. On that front, geography is on Iran’s side as it can fire at ships in the Strait of Hormuz from ground positions along its coast. It can also place sea mines in the strait using rockets to drop them .
These are tactics the US had not necessarily expected to contend with when it initiated the strikes on Iran but will certainly face now.
A mounting global energy crisis was not on the cards in February, but it is certainly looming now.
Disruptions to Gulf energy supplies caused by the war and fears of further escalation have pushed oil prices sharply higher. Brent crude, the global benchmark for oil prices, settled at $72.48 a barrel on February 27, the day before the war began.
It hit $126.41 on April 30, its highest level in four years. By early trading on Friday, it stood at $114.47.
But the crisis extends beyond crude oil . Damage and disruptions to fuel production in the Middle East alongside sanctions on Russian supplies have tightened diesel markets while the global supply of liquefied natural gas has also been heavily affected. The International Energy Agency warned in October that supply constraints could persist well into 2027.
Diesel, which has doubled in price in the US, is a big problem for Trump because farmers and businesses in the US, key voting blocs for him, rely on it for production and transport.
Alternative ports, pipelines and ship-to-ship transfers have helped Gulf producers restore exports but at higher logistical costs and with continuing exposure to attacks.
Re-establishing shipping through vital waterways in the Middle East, therefore, has now become a primary objective in the war.

### 40. ★★★☆☆ Europe needs a war economy

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T07:08:10+00:00
- **发生了什么**: With US support increasingly uncertain, Europe must mobilise its financial and industrial power to defend itself.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelenskyy speaks during a media conference at the EU summit in Brussels, Friday, Oct. 16, 2026. [Geert Vanden Wijngaert/AP Photo]
For the past four-and-a-half years, the war on Europe’s doorstep has haunted policymakers. Yet they have treated it as a looming threat rather than one already eroding the very foundations on which Europe stands.
Russia’s war has devastated much of Ukraine’s south and east, even as the front lines have shifted only slowly. For Europe, however, the challenge has never been confined to the battlefield. Supporting Ukraine and constraining Russia have tested the continent’s economic resources and political unity. Now, as United States President Donald Trump seeks to force a disadvantageous settlement on Kyiv and repeatedly threatens to withdraw US support, Europe faces the prospect of shouldering even more of that burden alone.
Even if the latest crisis is averted, Europe can no longer assume that Washington will guarantee its security. It must develop the financial capacity to sustain Ukraine’s defence, the industrial strength to defend itself and the economic leverage to constrain Moscow. To meet these challenges, Europe must build a war economy.
The past four years offer lessons in how Europe can finance such an effort, but also warnings about the divisions that could undermine it. The European Union has already been at the forefront of Ukraine’s recent financing, moving from an 18.1-billion-euro ($21bn) share of the G7’s $50bn Extraordinary Revenue Acceleration (ERA) loans in 2024 to finalising a new 90-billion-euro ($100bn) loan this April, one that came without US commitment. Yet internal divisions remain over numerous issues, particularly as they pertain to constraining Russia. This month, the EU adopted its largest-ever round of sanctions listings. But while the measures added a record number of designations, they did little to broaden the scope of restricted activity and did not accelerate the phase-out of Russian energy imports, with pipeline gas imports still permitted until autumn 2027. European leaders have also expressed concern over Trump’s decision to waive new Russia-related sanctions as he seeks to grapple with the costs his foreign policy has wrought.
However, the US-Israel war on Iran has, if anything, shown that Europe’s measures against Russian hydrocarbons could have gone further. Before February, some 20 million barrels per day (bpd) of crude and oil products and roughly 110 billion cubic metres (bcm) a year of liquefied natural gas (LNG) passed through the Strait of Hormuz. By comparison, Russia exported about 6.5 million bpd of oil and products in September, and some 43bcm of LNG last year – about a third of Hormuz’s oil and two-fifths of its LNG. As the crisis continues and Trump seeks additional Russian supplies, Europe must complement its efforts to constrain Moscow with greater investment in its own security.
The first and most important step is to establish a standing framework for joint EU borrowing – not only to finance Ukraine’s continued defence, but also to fund the defence industry Europe will need to sustain its own defence and, if necessary, prevail against future threats from Russia. Crisis begets innovation, as demonstrated during the COVID-19 pandemic, when EU member states pushed past their long-held aversion to mutualised borrowing, creating the 750-billion-euro NextGenerationEU recovery fund in 2020. The EU’s contribution to the ERA loans and this year’s 90-billion-euro facility, both financed by common EU borrowing, extended that logic to wartime finance. But these remain crisis-by-crisis improvisations. Deepening fiscal and capital markets union and building a genuine fiscal-military state would provide the foundations of Europe’s power and its ability to deter and win wars.
The same logic plays out in the US today, where deep capital markets have given companies such as SpaceX, which invested heavily for years before seeing returns, the space to develop technologies such as its Starlink network that have reshaped modern warfare. The importance of this technology is now keenly felt on Ukraine’s battlefields, where Kyiv’s communications have rested on a network subject to the whims of one man, SpaceX CEO Elon Musk. Europe therefore needs the fiscal capacity to fund its own technological development and procurement.
There are understandable jitters about the existing indebtedness of European states such as France and the United Kingdom. However, Russia’s frozen sovereign assets offer a potential way to support further borrowing. Divisions remain over using these assets, with Belgium, which hosts the lion’s share at Euroclear, opposing such a move over fears of Russian legal action and damage to its position in the global financial system. The bloc must come together to share that risk, which is overblown in any case . Not all EU fiscal support needs to come in the form of direct joint debt either. Greatly expanding the EU capital markets union and creating tax-advantaged structures across the region for defence firms and technological innovators may prove even more effective.
Beyond the assets themselves, Europe must also take greater control over its continued economic linkages with Russia. That means intervening far more actively in cases such as that of Austria’s Raiffeisen Bank. In 2025, following a Russian court ruling, more than 2.1 billion euros were taken from Raiffeisen’s Russian subsidiary to compensate Rasperia, a company tied to the sanctioned oligarch Oleg Deripaska. A Vienna court’s ruling last month now paves the way for Raiffeisen to seek compensation from Rasperia’s frozen assets in the EU – using the same sanctions derogation through which Euroclear released some $3bn in 2025. It is precisely this kind of legalised dealing with Moscow that keeps threats like those levelled at Belgium credible: if the Kremlin can seize European assets and prompt the release of Russian-linked assets frozen in the EU, every expropriation becomes a bargaining chip.
Furthermore, the UK and the EU have increasingly made use of measures against circumvention that share some features with US secondary sanctions. Europe should now be more assertive in making it clear that firms helping Russia evade its sanctions risk losing access to European financial systems and markets. US firms will choose Europe over the Kremlin, even if Trump waives more sanctions.
Finally, Europe should seek to reduce US influence over its foreign and security relationships. Speaking in Qatar this week, US Ambassador to Turkiye Tom Barrack said Washington was moving away from its decades-old bargain of security for Gulf oil. Europe may not be able to offer the same level of security, but it can offer a mutually beneficial partnership that addresses both sides’ concerns. And it is Europe that is now stepping up in the Gulf, with the UK and France  deploying jets, air defences and refuelling support to help Saudi Arabia against Houthi attacks. Unlike Washington, Europe can promise a deeper partnership in the future and serve as a reliable buyer of Gulf oil and gas exports in exchange for Gulf states doing more to address Europe’s security concerns about Russia through enhanced sanctions compliance and, first and foremost, ending the Kremlin’s role in OPEC plus.
Europe has the means to finance its own security. It must now find the will to borrow for it.
The views expressed in this article are the author’s own and do not necessarily reflect Al Jazeera’s editorial stance.

</details>
