### 41. ★★★☆☆ Andy Burnham warns of ‘winter of greater flooding’ – UK politics live

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T11:35:25+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The prime minister said the government is having conversation about emergency preparednessA DUP minister has said he is confident MP Carla Lockhart will stay in the party despite leaving its main decision-making body amid a row with party leadership over the future of Stormont.Lockhart is at odds with DUP leader, fellow MP Gavin Robinson, over whether the party should withdraw deputy first minister Emma Little-Pengelly from the power-sharing executive. <a hr

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Andy Burnham warns of ‘winter of greater flooding’ – UK politics live
The prime minister said the government is having conversation about emergency preparedness
Fri 23 Oct 2026 08.23 EDT First published on Fri 23 Oct 2026 04.01 EDT
Burnham: &#x27;Winter of great flooding&#x27; could be on its way
Britain needs to be on &#x27;front foot&#x27; on wildfires
Prime minister Andy Burnham meets members of the fire and rescue services during England's first wildfire resilience summit Photograph: Temilade Adelaja/PA
Prime minister Andy Burnham meets members of the fire and rescue services during England's first wildfire resilience summit Photograph: Temilade Adelaja/PA
As south west England deals with the aftermath of Storm Austen, Andy Burnham said the United Kingdom could face “a winter of greater flooding”.
The prime minister was asked if the Government was playing catch-up with civil preparedness as he attended a summit organised to learn from the summer’s record wildfires.
A second storm, Storm Boelo will bring disruption across the UK on Saturday.
Burnham told reporters on Friday: “No, we are having the conversation with people about being better prepared because we’re living in a world where unexpected events seem to be becoming more and more common.
“Obviously weather events – and we’re in a situation today where the UK is facing storms, and obviously we feel for the people without power in Cornwall right now – it is just having those supplies in the house to get you through those moments.
“So we are having that conversation about resilience. That’s why we’re holding this summit today, it’s why we’re putting the guidance out to the public.
“We potentially are facing a winter of greater flooding, so it’s about recognising all of those risks and taking action.”
Burnham: &#x27;Winter of great flooding&#x27; could be on its way
Britain needs to be on &#x27;front foot&#x27; on wildfires
Kemi Badenoch has waded into the preparedness debate and said ministers should “look after us” rather than just giving advice.
She told the Press Association during a visit to Cheshire constabulary headquarters that many people in rural areas already take precautions because they have no shops nearby.
Badenoch said: “We have power cuts on occasion, and if you’re in the middle of nowhere, it’s dark and cold. You do need to look after yourself.
“What I want to see is the government doing what it needs to do to look after us.
“The Defence Secretary is talking about stockpiling, but he won’t tell us if he’s found the money to pay for our defence.
“We need him to be looking after this country, manning the borders, ensuring the soldiers have all the equipment that they need.”
She later added: “I don’t mind them giving advice. I worry that between the prime minister being on Gogglebox and then talking about stockpiling canned goods, they’re not actually doing the really difficult stuff that will protect the country.”
In a comment piece, Polly Toynbee has said London Assembly member Andrew Boff, who resigned from the Conservative party this week, is a sign of where the party’s future should be.
The chair of the London Assembly, Andrew Boff, resigned this week from the Conservative party after 53 years of membership. He quit after being blocked from entering the race to be Tory candidate for London mayor because he supports the European convention on human rights (ECHR), putting him at odds with Kemi Badenoch’s hardline position.
Messages of support for one of the capital’s longest-serving Conservative politicians quickly poured in from fellow Tories. Gavin Barwell, Theresa May’s Downing Street chief of staff, has backed Boff, warning Badenoch that others would follow him over the ECHR edict. But the rest sent messages privately, not daring to reveal themselves under a leadership that doesn’t tolerate dissent. In an interview last year , Badenoch decreed that rebels “should not and cannot stand as a Conservative candidate” if they do not agree with leaving the ECHR. She also bars anyone supporting net zero .
When Boff applied, for the seventh time, to be considered as Conservative candidate for London mayor, he didn’t really expect to be selected – but nor did he expect to be summoned to be told he was barred from entering the race. In a statement he pointed to the fact that Winston Churchill helped found the ECHR after the second world war, arguing “it was never a leftwing project” but a “safeguard against arbitrary government”. What’s more, he points out to me, the “convention doesn’t even mention asylum seekers”, Badenoch’s main justification for leaving the treaty.
This man has quit Badenoch’s increasingly hard-right Tory party – others should be brave enough to do the same | Polly Toynbee
Andy Burnham has said the final decision on the Rosebank oil field will be “done properly” when asked about the potential for an approval to be overturned by the courts.
It comes after ministers gave the go-ahead to the Jackdaw gasfield, 150 miles east of Aberdeen in Scotland, after years of uncertainty and a court case.
The decision drew criticism from some Labour backbenchers and environmentalists, while Conservatives called on the government to also approve the Rosebank oilfield, west of Shetland in Scotland’s north.
Asked about the possibility of courts overturning a Rosebank approval, Burnham said on Friday: “Well, that’s a decision that will be taken in due course.
“What we have done this week is taken great care over the decision about Jackdaw because it wasn’t done properly in the past, and that led to the decision being struck down.
“What people can be certain about is things will be done properly going forward.
“And in relation to Jackdaw, people will question: Was that the right decision? It is going to be a bridge to a renewable future?
“The funds that it will release will be able to go into building out that clean energy system that the country needs, which is a further part of our national resilience going forward.”
A DUP minister has said he is confident MP Carla Lockhart will stay in the party despite leaving its main decision-making body amid a row with party leadership over the future of Stormont.
Lockhart is at odds with DUP leader, fellow MP Gavin Robinson, over whether the party should withdraw deputy first minister Emma Little-Pengelly from the power-sharing executive.
Lockhart has argued that the party should pull Little-Pengelly from her post as it would automatically remove Sinn Féin first minister Michelle O’Neill from her position in the joint office they lead together.
Such a step would effectively collapse the devolved administration in Belfast. However other individual departmental ministers would remain in post and the Northern Ireland assembly would continue to sit for a further six months.
Lockhart believes the party should no longer serve alongside O’Neill after the Sinn Féin vice-president took part in an allegedly unlawful protest on the Garvaghy Road in Portadown last month.
Education minister Paul Given told BBC Radio Ulster: “Carla bleeds DUP just like I do, and of course it’s disappointing that she felt not able to remain as a party officer.
“But I very much welcome her remaining within the DUP because she is a valued member of our party, and I know that she’ll continue to make an important contribution to the party and in leading people in Upper Bann.”
There has been a few lighthearted moments from Andy Burnham’s comments to the press today.
Burnham will appear on Channel 4’s Gogglebox tonight as part of a Stand Up to Cancer fundraiser.
According to the Press Association, when asked about his views on the Celebrity Traitors so far, the prime minister said: “Well, obviously, I would say that politicians have got a lot to offer to those who are looking for tactics in relation to The Traitors.”
Burnham, who seized power from Sir Keir Starmer after plotting to be prime minister for more than a year, added: “I think we can spot faithfuls and traitors a mile off.
“Our profession teaches us to do that, but it’s a great programme.
“It’s a great example of how strong British broadcasting is, and of course the BBC.”
Meanwhile he responded to housing secretary Angela Rayner’s suggestion wine and mascara would be in her emergency supplies kit, after the government recommended people to prepare, Burnham said: “I’m not sure that that’s the top of the list in terms of the advice that we’re giving to the to the public.
“You need to have a clear head when it comes to a challenging situation.
“So yeah, some tins of food, some bottled water. But I’m sure people are allowed a glass or two as well to get them through a difficult situation.”
As south west England deals with the aftermath of Storm Austen, Andy Burnham said the United Kingdom could face “a winter of greater flooding”.
The prime minister was asked if the Government was playing catch-up with civil preparedness as he attended a summit organised to learn from the summer’s record wildfires.
A second storm, Storm Boelo will bring disruption across the UK on Saturday.
Burnham told reporters on Friday: “No, we are having the conversation with people about being better prepared because we’re living in a world where unexpected events seem to be becoming more and more common.
“Obviously weather events – and we’re in a situation today where the UK is facing storms, and obviously we feel for the people without power in Cornwall right now – it is just having those supplies in the house to get you through those moments.
“So we are having that conversation about resilience. That’s why we’re holding this summit today, it’s why we’re putting the guidance out to the public.
“We potentially are facing a winter of greater flooding, so it’s about recognising all of those risks and taking action.”
Andy Burnham is addressing the wildfire summit in north west England after a summer which saw homes destroyed by fires during the record-breaking heat.
Burnham visited Stourbridge, in the West Midlands, days after fires wreaked havoc in streets on the edge of the Black Country town in August.
He said Britain needs to be on the “front foot” and that the country had been playing “catch up” for too long.
The prime minister said he expected similar summers going forward.
He told the summit: “I think too often in the past we’ve been playing catch up, and today is trying about trying to get on the front foot and recognise that the summer we’ve just had is likely to be a more regular situation going forward. So make ourselves that bit stronger when it comes to the summer of 2027, and then indeed summers beyond.”
He earlier told journalists: “We lived through a summer that we’ve never seen before, with so many wildfires across the country. Fire and rescue services were stretched, firefighters working extremely difficult conditions, and it was right in that moment to commit to this summit, and today we’re making good on that, but also making some initial steps to improve our response because we will have to plan for summers like this going forward.
“So firstly: better equipment for our firefighters, particularly the PPE, more appropriate to working in very hot summer conditions because it’s very punishing work what they did this summer.
“Secondly, aerial support: so we may see on holiday other countries where there are planes putting water from above on the fires. We believe we need that capability. The Ministry of Defence will be equipping aircraft with that capability, so we’ll be better prepared for summers to come.
“Then thirdly: just better management of the land, so that fire doesn’t get the ability to spread. Those are the early commitments, but the summit will give us more as we go through it today, and people can be sure that we will be even better prepared next summer.”
Andy Burnham has said he believes indefinite leave to remain needs to be “earned through contribution”, but stopped short of committing to all of home secretary Shabana Mahmood’s immigration and citizenship reforms.
There has been speculation that Mahmood’s plans to double the amount of time care workers will need to live in Britain before claiming indefinite leave to remain will be watered down under pressure from Labour MPs.
Currently the wait is five years, but Mahmood said it should be raised to ten.
Burnham said: “We’re in a position where we’ve been consulting on those reforms, and we will come forward with our decisions soon.
“But I strongly support the Home Secretary when she says that settlement is a right to be earned through contribution, and that will be the principle that guides our reforms.”
Foreign secretary Ed Miliband is in Turkey today as he is set to join talks on the strait of Hormuz and the situation in Saudi Arabia.
Ministers from Britain, France and Germany as well as Italy, will meet in Turkey to discuss issues in the Middle East and the war in Ukraine.
Turkey, Saudi Arabia, Pakistan and Egypt will also attend.
The UK said this week it would extend its military support for the Saudis, providing British fighter jets to shoot down Houthi drones and missiles fired into the kingdom.
Miliband will use the talks to point to Iranian tactics being replicated, including with Houthi attacks on Saudi Arabia putting pressure on the maritime choke points of Bab El-Mandeb and the Suez canal.
Andy Burnham is “determined” to introduce the tourist tax across the country and could overrule councils and elected mayors, the Times has reported.
Burnham told an online meeting of mayors he wanted to counter the “negative” atmosphere around the overnight visitor levy. It has received some opposition from the hospitality sector and some mayors.
According to the Times , the prime minister told the National Economic Council: “If the mayor’s name is linked to a tax, it shouldn’t be the case that they’re not able then to pursue what is a mayoral ambition.
“So I think the rewiring of the state needs to clean out this a bit more as we go forward … We’ve got to get the brakes off growth basically and that’s something I’m determined to do.”
As Wes Streeting and James Murray have been recommending in the last 24 hours, the Government is advising people to stockpile supplies for three days.
Have you been persuaded to follow this advice? The Guardian would like to speak to you about what you have in your cupboard, and what other items you have in case of emergency.
Tell us: are you stockpiling supplies in case of an emergency?
British Army veteran Steve Knighton holds a collection tin as British prime minister Andy Burnham makes a donation to the Poppy Appeal on 22 October. Photograph: Christopher Furlong/Getty Images Andy Burnham has been meeting armed forces veterans and Royal British Legion Poppy Appeal collectors to mark the start of the year’s campaign.
Burnham welcomed them to Number 10 North’s office in central Manchester, contrary to the usual convention of making a donation to the cause on the steps of Number 10 in Downing Street.
Good morning from London, where we’re less than a week from Andy Burnham and John Healey’s first budget, and the Government has been telling people to build up 72 hours of supplies in case of a disaster. We’ll be bringing you updates throughout the day. I’m Harry Taylor.
The Government has said that will not be putting together a “shopping list” for the public to buy in anticipation of a disaster, despite launching a campaign on Thursday which told people to stock up on supplies.
Treasury minister James Murray has been on the broadcast round this morning where has been asked about the announcement yesterday that people should assemble three days of supplies.
It’s come after repeated warnings in the last 18 months about cyber-attacks from overseas that could affect key systems.
It’s also timely as 32 Government services were taken offline yesterday after millions of attempts to exploit a weakness in numerous’ websites coding.
However Murray told Times Radio this morning: “This is not about the Government sending out a shopping list to everyone in the country.
“This is about putting the right information there on the government website, having an information campaign so that people are aware about it and then people taking the right choices for themselves about what they need to make sure they have supplies of at home.”
Meanwhile it’s five days until Healey’s budget, and Murray was asked on BBC Radio 4 whether he would be reducing red tape for business.
He said cutting “pointless bureaucracy” was “at the heart of what the Chancellor is going to be thinking about”.
Healey said the Treasury would aim to cut £800m of red tape for businesses at the budget, including reduced reporting requirements.

France to deploy 70,000 officers as it braces for weekend of protests – Europe live
Interior minister Laurent Nuñez defended blanket bans on demonstrations in some parts of France
Fri 23 Oct 2026 08.15 EDT First published on Fri 23 Oct 2026 03.55 EDT
France protest bans criticised by the left and civil rights groups
70,000 police officers and gendarmes to be deployed
One dead, four injured in shooting in town west of Paris
Austria expels four more Russian diplomats suspected of spying
Students stage sit-in protest at Sciences Po Paris on Thursday Photograph: Luc Auffret/Anadolu/Getty Images
Students stage sit-in protest at Sciences Po Paris on Thursday Photograph: Luc Auffret/Anadolu/Getty Images
The French interior minister says the government is mobilising 70,000 police officers and gendarmes- military officers who serve in the national police force – to city streets this weekend.
Riot squads and other elite, specialised units will be deployed. Even police trainees are being drafted in as support.
Laurent Nuñez said the government was aiming for an “extremely mobile, agile and effective” force. Some units will be stationed specifically outside government and public buildings as protection.
As I’ve noted earlier, several museums and tourist sites are closing this weekend – including Paris’s major museums the Louvre and Orsay – while shops and businesses are also boarding up.
France protest bans criticised by the left and civil rights groups
70,000 police officers and gendarmes to be deployed
One dead, four injured in shooting in town west of Paris
Austria expels four more Russian diplomats suspected of spying
In Ukraine , Volodymyr ‌Zelenskyy has said that a large-scale swap of prisoners has taken place with Russia.
Ukraine on Friday ⁠brought ⁠home 270 people, ​including nine civilians.
Ukrainian prisoners of war back in their country after a prisoner swap with Russia. Photograph: Ukrainian Presidential Press Service/Reuters President Emmanuel Macron has commented on a shooting earlier today in the town of Trappes, west of Paris, which has left one person dead and wounded four others, including a 12-year-old girl.
We would like to thank our ​law enforcement agencies, which have ‌already taken the ‌suspect into custody. The National Anti-Terrorism Prosecutor’s Office has taken ‌over the case.
French police work at the scene after a drive-by shooting in Trappes. Photograph: Stéphanie Lecocq/Reuters It’s all a bit of a moveable feast at the moment in terms of where and when protests can or cannot be held this weekend.
Initially authorities in cities like Marseille and Lyon had issued blanket bans on any unauthorised demonstrations.
That prompted 1.) a rush by trade unions and student groups to file their protest plans with officials and negotiate march routes and
2.) last-minute legal challenges with plaintiffs arguing their constitutional right to protest is being suppressed by the government.
Trade unionists, lawyers, students, public intellectuals, and rights activists have all come out criticising the curbs on protest. The government says it’s trying to prevent a repeat of the looting, fires and violence seen last weekend.
Left-wing politicians also denounced the “headlong authoritarian rush” of the government. Far-left leader Jean-Luc Mélenchon’s party put out this statement this morning:
By deciding to ban demonstrations, to choose who has the right to organize them, to put in place curfews on the scale of a department or a city, to encourage traders to use private security, the executive is crossing one by one the limits that separate a democracy from an authoritarian and police state.
Furthermore, bans on demonstrations are foolish decisions that are likely to generate chaos. Indeed, these bans will not quell anger against the government’s policies, nor will they quell the desire to express it in the streets.
Meanwhile co-president of the Association of Criminal Lawyers, Romain Boulet, slammed the thousands of arrests that have occurred this week calling it: “The most repressive week in the history of the Fifth Republic.”
Never before in France have there been so many arrests and so many people brought before the courts as this week.
The Justice Minister has just given an updated on sentencings- saying 232 people have been sentenced to prison since Saturday.
Elite units like the GIGN – the National Gendarmerie Intervention Group- and RAID (Research, Assistance, Intervention and Deterrence) are being activated tomorrow in the protests.
These units more commonly specialise in counter-terrorism events, hostage rescue, protecting senior state officials.
Nuñez defended their involvement in policing these street demonstrations of students, workers and civil servants. He noted calls on social media “from the far-left” to target ​public buildings.
“We are not talking about ⁠a peaceful, friendly demonstration,” Nunez ​told reporters. “We ​are ​ready tomorrow ​to be ‌extremely firm ​and ​not tolerate anything.“
A wall of police counter student demonstrators at a rally in central Paris on Thursday, October 22, 2026. Photograph: Abdul Saboor/Reuters The French interior minister says the government is mobilising 70,000 police officers and gendarmes- military officers who serve in the national police force – to city streets this weekend.
Riot squads and other elite, specialised units will be deployed. Even police trainees are being drafted in as support.
Laurent Nuñez said the government was aiming for an “extremely mobile, agile and effective” force. Some units will be stationed specifically outside government and public buildings as protection.
As I’ve noted earlier, several museums and tourist sites are closing this weekend – including Paris’s major museums the Louvre and Orsay – while shops and businesses are also boarding up.
Nuñez again condemned the vandalism and looting seen on the sidelines of protests last weekend – saying authorities would be “uncompromising” with “the vandals, with the looters and with all those who will commit atrocities”.
He also again repeated that some of this had been stoked by groups “on the far-left and radical extreme left” – but didn’t provide details.
“What happened on Octobe 17 is totally unacceptable. We will be fully in control to prevent these lootings, these attacks on public buildings” from occurring again, he said.
More than 200 people have already been jailed over last weekend’s violence.
French interior minister Laurent Nuñez is speaking now following a meeting of the Defence Council this morning, chaired by President Macron.
He begins by mentioning the shooting this morning in Trappes before going on to defending some of the blanket bans on demonstrations that have been issued in different regions across the country- saying each prefecture has the best idea of the security situation.
For example, until an hour ago, Marseille authorities had banned all demonstrations but the prefecture there has now allowed a trade union march to go ahead tomorrow.
Several local protest bans are also being challenged in last-minute court hearings today.
Nuñez will also be travelling to Orleans tonight to speak with law enforcement there ahead of the National Rally congress and counter-protests expected in the city tomorrow.
The French prime minister Sébastien Lecornu has just issued a statement on a shooting in a town west of Paris this morning where one person was killed and four others injured.
Police said a gunman had opened fire in Trappes, in the Yvelines, and neighbouring La Verriere. The gunman was later arrested in nearby Coignieres.
Romania’s President Nicusor Dan has just now also blamed Russia for the Black Sea ship strike, where one sailor was killed and three others are still missing.
“This is clearly part of the escalation that Russia is carrying out in the context of the war in Ukraine ,” Dan told reporters.
French Interior Minister Laurent Nuñez is due to hold a press conference shortly on the specific measures for protesters this weekend.
We’ll carry a live stream of that at the top of the page.
There’s been a major crackdown on demonstrators; police have arrested 2,700 people since last weekend, Justice Minister Gerald Darmanin said yesterday. He added that courts have also already sent 217 people to prison– half of them first-time offenders.
I reported earlier Russia’s claim to having struck a cargo vessel in the Black Sea.
Romanian authorities are saying this morning that one person has been killed and three people are missing from a ship in the Black Sea.
They sent a rescue operation after the Khoryv vessel sent a distress signal around 0700 GMT while 13 nautical miles from the Romanian port of Constanta. There were 10 Ukrainian and three Azerbijani nationals in the crew, Romanian officials said.
“Rescue vessels have managed to recover nine people alive, and one body from the water,” the Romanian Naval Authority said, adding three others were missing.
There’s been a wave of drone attacks on merchant vessels in the Black Sea off Romania and Bulgaria in recent weeks. Romanian fighter jets shot down two Russian drones over the sea last weekend and NATO said this week it would deploy more planes and radar systems to the area.
Bulgaria is also holding a vote for the mainly ceremonial presidential role on Sunday. Election campaigns have mainly been focused on Russia’s war in Ukraine and the widening hostilities in the Black Sea.
Hot days pushed vulnerable bodies into overdrive and warm nights prevented them from recovering. Photograph: Tom Nicholson/Reuters I’m going to track away from the French protests just slightly – although one of the demands of Parisian high schoolers is for air-conditioning and other basics in their classrooms – and highlight some shocking new numbers on just how many people died from the extreme heatwaves across Europe this summer.
Researchers from Imperial and London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine say the heat killed a record 50,000 people.
People succumbed to heat at the highest rates in Malta, Italy and Hungary, while absolute death tolls were greatest in Italy, France, Spain, Germany and the UK.
The “staggering” death toll, modelled using historical relationships between heat and health in 854 cities, is the worst on record.
Scientists are blaming climate breakdown for two in every three of the deaths. The share of deaths due to climate breakdown has risen from about 40% at the start of the century to 65% this summer, the study found – leading to an extra 32,000 deaths.
Because death certificates rarely record contributing factors – such as dangerous temperatures that push a stroke victim or lung cancer patient past their limits – families rarely know that heat and human activity hastened their relatives’ death.
Heat killed ‘staggering’ 50,000 people in Europe’s cities this summer, scientists warn
But Macron’s government has ramped up restrictions and protest curbs, to try and stop the looting and ransacking we saw on the fringes of the wider peaceful demonstrations last week.
Critics this morning such as civil rights groups, unionists and lawyers, are lambasting the “repressive” measures.
Both student groups and trade unions have planned rallies across the country. France’s ⁠main student union, UNEF, has called for demonstrations on Saturday and the Yellow Vest movement also filed plans to march through central Paris.
Marine Le Pen is also hosting the far-right National Rally congress in the central city Orléans, so we’re anticipating protests there with up to 30,000 counter-demonstrators expected.
There are already overnight curfews in place in Paris and many other cities.
In Marseille and Lyon, the second and third-largest cities, there is a general ban on demonstrations however some union groups have been authorised to hold marches.
The Marseille city government also initially said it would stop public transport running on Saturday; they’ve hastily reversed that severely unpopular decision this morning.
In Paris, protesters have been told they can only march a designated route between the Bastille and Nation squares.
Le Monde has this handy map on their site with all the curbs and restrictions and local curfew times
Many shops and businesses, tourist sites and events are boarding up and closing for the weekend. Marseille’s business council advised shops to do so.
In Paris, the Louvre Museum will be shut while the Art Basel Paris art fair is also finishing up early.
On top of the protest bans and restrictions in place, the government says they’ll deploy 20% more police onto the streets this weekend
Macron held a Security Council meeting on Thursday with senior ministers; he’s chairing a Defence Council meeting this morning discussing other measures with law enforcement
This afternoon the French president is also due to host student protest leaders for the first time for negotiations at the Elysee.
Macron, center, attends a security council with France’s Interior Minister Laurent Nunez, left, and France’s Prime Minister Sebastien Lecornu, center left, at the Elysee Palace in Paris, Thursday, Oct. 22, 2026. Photograph: Suarez Teresa/ABACA/Shutterstock In Vienna this morning, Austria’s foreign ministry has announced it is kicking out another four Russian diplomats over “activities incompatible ⁠with their diplomatic status” – a euphemism usually for espionage.
Three of them worked at the Russian embassy while the other worked at the Organization ​for ​Security and Cooperation in Europe) in arms control, Die Presse is reporting.
They had all been monitored for some time by the Austrian security authorities.
You have to wonder how many Russian diplomats are left in Vienna – Europe’s espionage capital since the cold war, as my colleague Jon Henley puts it. Just a few months ago, Austria also expelled three Russian embassy staff on spying allegations, after a “forest of antennae” was detected at the Russian mission.
Austria has expelled now 18 Russian diplomats since 2020.
A quick update from what the Ukrainian and Russian officials are saying this morning on overnight attacks:
Ukraine says Russia targeted energy sites in six different regions. Currently more than 500,000 people are without power.
Russia’s defence ministry confirmed strikes on Vilkovo port in Odesa, a railcar depot in Poltava and a cargo vessel in the Black Sea.
A war of words has erupted between the Trump administration , officials in Kyiv and Elon Musk over who is to blame for limiting Ukraine’s access to Space X’s military-grade version of Starlink.
The restrictions to Starshield have rendered many of the drones used by Kyiv to strike behind the frontlines inoperable and hampered air defence systems that stop Russian attacks, according to Ukrainian officials .
The US president said on Thursday that the service was running again at full speed, but a European official speaking to the Associated Press denied the service was fully functioning.
Trump initially denied any responsibility for the changes to Starshield, telling reporters last Friday: “I don’t know if it was anybody, but it wasn’t me, and I don’t think it was Elon,” referring to Musk, SpaceX’s CEO.
Trump’s cabinet backed him up, with secretary of state Marco Rubio saying on Wednesday: “What I can tell you with certainty is the president of the United States nor anyone in our system ever requested that any changes to that system be made.”
Rubio, however, also made a comment that threw suspicion on to Musk, the world’s richest man.
The president has nothing to do with the decisions that are made by Starlink and Starshield and by Mr Musk. That’s a private company. The president doesn’t run that company and can’t direct that company to do anything.
The Wall Street Journal reported on Thursday that the US president had asked Musk to restore the service, with Trump later telling reporters the system was “back”.
He refused to comment on whether he had spoken to Musk, but appeared to suggest the ultimate power to limit the service rested with the SpaceX CEO. “I guess the bottom line, he put it back,” Trump said, referring to Musk.
However, John Cornyn, a Republican who is in his final weeks as the US senator for Texas, told US media that he didn’t believe Musk would have acted “without at least the acquiescence of the White House”.
Musk has supported Trump’s calls for Volodymyr Zelenskyy to be replaced and has been highly critical of the Ukrainian president. However, he was quick to blame the limits on Starshield on the Trump administration, writing on X last week that the systems were “owned and controlled by the US government”.
Any limits on speed or usage are decided by the US military and ultimately POTUS.
Musk – one of the Republican party’s biggest donors and a Trump ally – continued to deny responsibility for the changes. However, on Tuesday the Wall Street Journal reported that Musk made the order to slow down Starshield, with the paper’s sources in disagreement over whether this was made in concert with Trump.
Vice-president JD Vance backed Musk on Thursday, saying the billionaire “doesn’t want to get in the middle of what is a war between two state actors without the United States government effectively directing him on what to do”.
So I don’t think this is about Elon. I think it’s fundamentally what is America’s policy in Ukraine and Russia going to be.
Amid the war of words, civilian casualties in Ukraine have surged , and the question over who is ultimately responsible for limiting Kyiv’s access has remained unanswered.
Trump, Musk, Zelenskyy: who is blaming who in the Ukraine-Starlink row?
Good morning and welcome to our Europe live blog, where we’ll begin in Ukraine .
Local authorities reported explosions overnight in Lutsk, a city not far from the Polish border, and in the central Poltava and southern Sumy regions. Russia this morning has also claimed strikes on ships in the Black Sea .
Both sides engaged in heavy strikes yesterday with Russia bombarding Kyiv with ballistic missiles and Ukraine sending swarms of drones to Moscow.
Trump has declared that Starlink is once again running at full speed across Ukraine after the service was throttled last week -but Ukrainian and European officials say they haven’t seen that speed and functionality resume.
The satellite service was still only operating at a cap of 161km/hr, crippling key air defences and limiting mid-range strikes, a Ukrainian official told the Kyiv Independent after Trump’s comments. “Overall, nothing has changed anywhere,” the paper quoted the unnamed official saying.
Elsewhere we’re watching this continued wave of student-led protests across Europe. From Rome to Athens, Madrid and Paris , young people are venting their anger over the lack of resources in schools as well as wider housing and cost-of-living concerns. They’ve often been joined by their teachers in these ralies.
Fearing another weekend of street marches descending into violence and looting, French authorities have banned some protests over the coming days. President Emmanuel Macron , just returned from his mid-week tour to the Gulf states, is due to meet with student leaders around lunchtime in Paris.
It’s Frances Mao today in London, covering for Jakub, stay with me for all the developments.

The Institute for the Study of War (ISW) and The Critical Threats Project (CTP) at the American Enterprise Institute are publishing updates Monday through Friday to provide analysis on the war with Iran and the broader Middle East. Weekday updates cover events from the past 24-hour period, whereas weekend updates are abbreviated and cover the Houthi-Saudi conflict and major breaking developments related to the war with Iran.
The Houthis almost certainly captured a major roadway segment that links Houthi frontlines on the outskirts of Taiz City to other frontlines in central Taiz Governorate on October 22. The Houthis posted footage of fighters in vehicles at various geolocated positions along a segment of the Taiz-Aden ground line of communication (GLOC) in the al Dhabab valley between al Misrakh District and Taiz City's southwestern limits.
The United States and Israel are reportedly making preparations for renewed strikes on Iran after the US midterm elections. The New York Times reported on October 21 that the US and Israeli militaries have developed specific plans for a short and intensive operation against Iran after the US midterm elections on November 3, citing US officials and other people briefed on the deliberations.
The Houthis almost certainly captured a major roadway segment that links Houthi frontlines on the outskirts of Taiz City to other frontlines in central Taiz Governorate on October 22. The Houthis posted footage of fighters in vehicles at various geolocated positions along a segment of the Taiz-Aden ground line of communication (GLOC) in the al Dhabab valley between al Misrakh District and Taiz City's southwestern limits.[1] These geolocated positions indicate that the Houthis have effectively secured the Taiz-Aden GLOC between al Turbah in southern Taiz Governorate and the outskirts of Taiz City.[2] The Houthis can exploit control over this roadway to quickly transport supplies and reinforcements between frontlines surrounding Taiz City and other combat areas further south in al Misrakh District.[3] The Houthis are simultaneously attempting to capture favorable terrain on Taiz City's western outskirts and prevent Yemeni government-aligned forces in al Misrakh District from breaking out of the Houthis' encirclement. The Houthis could use this newly obtained GLOC segment to reinforce positions on either front.[4]
Houthi fighters also likely captured the Hangar Checkpoint, which lies along a key roadway through Taiz City’s southwestern limits, according to geolocated footage released on October 22.[5] The Houthis have repeatedly attacked Yemeni government-aligned forces at this checkpoint in recent days, likely in order to secure a roadway into Taiz's city center from the southwest.[6] The Houthis also posted footage of fighters at a geolocated position on Jabal Han, which is a mountain that overlooks western Taiz City and provides favorable positions for observing and targeting nearby Yemeni government-aligned forces.[7] ISW-CTP cannot assess whether the Houthis have consolidated control over Jabal Han at the time of this writing because the Yemeni government has repeatedly recaptured parts of the mountain following brief Houthi advances in recent weeks.[8]
The United States and Israel are reportedly making preparations for renewed strikes on Iran after the US midterm elections. The New York Times reported on October 21 that the US and Israeli militaries have developed specific plans for a short and intensive operation against Iran after the US midterm elections on November 3, citing US officials and other people briefed on the deliberations.[9] US officials added that US Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth met with Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu in recent days to discuss renewed strikes.[10] Axios, citing US and Israeli officials, reported on October 19 that dozens of Israeli fighter jets and aerial refueling tankers executed a sortie through Syrian and Iraqi airspace and turned back just as they reached the Iranian border.[11] Some US and Israeli officials stated that the sortie sought to deter Iran from attacking Israel ahead of Israel’s elections on October 27.[12]
Iranian officials and media have recently threatened to expand their target base if the United States and Israel strike Iran, including threatening strikes on Gulf undersea cable infrastructure.[13] Iran also reportedly conducted over 30 tests of medium-range solid-fuel Emad and Kheibar Shikan ballistic missiles on October 11, almost certainly in preparation for a possible renewed conflict with the United States and Israel.[14]
Several sources, including an Israeli media outlet and an open-source intelligence (OSINT) account, claimed on October 22 that US forces struck Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) missile launchers near Sirik, Hormozgan Province.[15] US CENTCOM has not confirmed any US strikes in Iran as of this writing. The exact target of the IRGC’s attempted missile launch is unclear, assuming the reports are accurate. Iranian forces have targeted commercial vessels in the Strait of Hormuz at an increased rate over the past month in order to reestablish control of the strait. ISW-CTP assessed on October 21 that Iran may be using more accurate systems, such as weapons systems with infrared terminal guidance, to attack some of these ships.[16] The regime previously faced targeting constraints in August and early September 2026 as a result of US strikes on Iranian coastal radar infrastructure.[17] It is also possible that Iran attempted to target US naval forces. Iran fired a number of ballistic and anti-ship cruise missiles targeting US naval forces in early September 2026, but ISW-CTP has not observed any Iranian attempts to target US naval forces since.[18]
Secure international recognition of Iranian control over the Strait of Hormuz
Degrade the US ability and willingness to continue the war
Restore deterrence vis- à -vis the United States and its regional partners
Divide the United States and Israel from the Arab states
The US naval blockade on Iranian ports has degraded Iran’s ability to earn revenue from oil exports, which may motivate the Iranian regime to intensify efforts designed to break the US blockade. The Wall Street Journal reported on October 21, citing oil analysis firm Kpler, that Iran has exhausted the oil reserves that it has stored on vessels anchored beyond the US blockade line and previously sold to the People’s Republic of China to earn revenue.[19] US Central Command (CENTCOM) stated on October 17 that the blockade has denied the regime at least $25 billion USD in oil revenue.[20] Oil revenue is vital to the regime’s operations, which include reconstituting its ballistic missile and drone programs.[21] Increasing costs imposed by the US naval blockade may motivate the Iranian regime to intensify military efforts designed to break the US will or ability to maintain the blockade. The IRGC Aerospace Force claimed on September 5 that it targeted a US aircraft carrier and destroyer that were enforcing the US naval blockade, for example.[22]
The Iraqi federal government has continued its efforts to prevent Iranian-backed militia attacks on neighboring countries from Iraqi territory, likely in response to US and Saudi pressure. Unspecified sources told Iraqi media on October 22 that Iraqi authorities have increased surveillance in the past two weeks over militia sites in Maysan Province that the authorities suspect militias are using to conduct attacks.[23] The Iraqi media report assessed that the Houthis may be involved in operations at these sites and also noted the involvement of “shadow militias,” probably referring to militia cells that the IRGC directly manages.[24] IRGC-managed Iraqi militia cells based along the Iraq-Iran border, including in Maysan Province, have conducted multiple attacks on neighboring countries in recent months, including a September 10 attack on Saudi Arabia’s East-West Pipeline.[25] The Houthis, who maintain an active presence in Iraq, reportedly coordinated some of these cross-border attacks with Iraqi militias.[26] Baghdad temporarily closed multiple border crossings on the Iraq-Iran border in response to the attacks and dismissed security officials who were allegedly connected to the attacks.[27] The Iraqi federal government has more recently taken some steps to limit the Houthis’ presence in Iraq, including by ordering the closure of unspecified Houthi-owned offices in Iraq on October 21.[28]
Yemeni government-aligned forces are continuing to steadily consolidate control over terrain on the southern end of the Red Sea coastal plain. Anti-Houthi Yemeni media and independent Yemeni journalists reported on October 22 that the Southern Giants Brigades and Homeland Shield Forces, which are government-aligned military factions, reestablished full control over the al Aradi military camp on the southern end of the Red Sea coastal plain.[29] These claims suggest that at least some Houthi fighters are continuing to contest control of terrain by fighting government-aligned forces in areas south of Dhubab Airport, which is the site of the Yemeni government's northernmost advance on the coastal plain.[30] Al Aradi is of high tactical significance because it lies astride the Bab al Mandeb Strait, where the Houthis could fire close-range weapons at civilian vessels in the Strait if they were to maintain control over the area.[31] The Houthis still pose a broader operational threat to international maritime traffic in the Bab al Mandeb and Red Sea, however, because the group maintains control over most of the coastal plain and possesses weapons with sufficient range to attack vessels throughout the waterway at any time.[32]
The Houthis are continuing to attack Saudi government, civilian, and oil infrastructure sites in order to compel Saudi leaders to withdraw support for the Yemeni government and capitulate to Houthi demands. Houthi military spokesperson Yahya Saree announced on October 22 that the Houthis launched ballistic missiles and drones targeting a Saudi Aramco oil site in Yanbu, Abha International Airport, King Fahd International Airport in Dammam, and the Saudi Defense Ministry (MOD) headquarters in Riyadh.[33] The Houthis' claimed strike on the Saudi MOD headquarters is the first Houthi attempt to attack a Saudi ministry headquarters since the current conflict began in July 2026. Saudi authorities also reported on October 21 and 22 that Saudi air defenses intercepted Houthi drones over Riyadh, Medina, and a Saudi Aramco oil processing plant at the Shaybah Oil Field, Eastern Province.[34] The Houthis' high volume and broad geographic scope of the targeting is consistent with Houthi efforts in recent weeks to expand their strike campaign against Saudi airports, oil facilities, and other infrastructure.[35] The Houthis calculate that they can compel Saudi Arabia to withdraw support for the Yemeni government and capitulate to other Houthi demands by maximizing the kingdom's domestic political and economic costs.[36]
The Yemeni government announced on October 21 that it will begin attacking Houthi public assemblies, recruitment events, and tribal gatherings, almost certainly to hinder Houthi efforts to sustain the group’s military forces and public support.[37] The Yemeni government is almost certainly responding to the Houthis' strike campaign against staging areas and training camps of Yemeni government-aligned forces, which has sought to disrupt the government's counteroffensives in southern and eastern Yemen.[38] The Houthis routinely use tribal gatherings and other public assemblies to recruit and mobilize fighters, including child soldiers, to support the group's offensive efforts across frontlines throughout Yemen.[39] The Yemeni government's announcement is likely in part an informational effort to deter Yemeni tribes and civilians from cooperating with the Houthis or providing manpower for the Houthi war effort. The Yemeni government’s intent to strike public assemblies and tribal gatherings also represents a discrete government effort to disrupt Houthi recruitment, training, and reinforcement.

### 45. ★★★☆☆ The Iran war’s toxic impact on one of the planet’s most unique seas - CNN

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T08:46:57+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The Iran war’s toxic impact on one of the planet’s most unique seas&nbsp;&nbsp;CNN

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The conflict in the Middle East is shaping up to be yet another US war with a toxic legacy .
Bombs and missiles have released heavy metals and harmful chemicals into the atmosphere. US-Israeli and Iranian strikes have hit oil facilities, igniting fires that covered Tehran in thick black smoke and oil-saturated rain , exposing millions to pollution which could have long-lasting health impacts.
And these strikes are also affecting the Persian Gulf. Attacks on ships and oil infrastructure in the waterway have produced oil spills visible from space .
A spill from a tanker has temporarily closed beaches in the Emirate of Umm Al Quwain with authorities working to contain the environmental impacts, according to an announcement Thursday by the United Arab Emirates Ministry of Climate Change and Environment.
It’s not been confirmed whether the spill is linked to Iranian attacks but it’s yet another sign of the huge risks faced by the Persian Gulf, one of the world’s most delicate and unique marine systems.
As the conflict continues, experts are warning of a broader contamination crisis.
Go beyond the forecast with stunning stories and visuals.
For months, cargo ships and oil tankers have been targeted in the Strait of Hormuz, the vital shipping route for oil and gas between the Persian Gulf and the Gulf of Oman.
The progression from vessel impact to environmental damage is clear to see in many cases, said Brian Barnes, who is part of a group of satellite oceanographers at the University of South Florida analyzing the effects of this war from afar.
“Some of the major ships that have been targeted, we see prolonged plumes, stretching for hundreds of kilometers away from the impact site,” Barnes said.
The Persian Gulf has long suffered from oil pollution, but research by Barnes and other scientists comparing March 2026, when fighting was intense, with the same period last year shows a sharp increase.
An oil tanker burns after being hit by an Iranian strike in the ship-to-ship transfer zone at Khor al-Zubair port near Basra, Iraq, March 11, 2026.
“What we’re seeing is twice the amount of surface oil in the Persian Gulf area relative to what we would expect in the absence of a major conflict,” Barnes said.
Although, he noted, satellite imagery can only show what is on the surface of the water and not the thickness of the oil, which would give a more accurate picture of the extent of the contamination.
What is harder to assess with limited physical access to the conflict-riddled waterway is the impact on marine wildlife.
The Persian Gulf hosts an ecosystem teeming with life. It is home to several species of sea turtles, hundreds of species of fish and the world’s second-largest population of dugongs, or sea cows.
“One of the world’s largest whale shark congregations occurs close to the South Pars oil field where Qatar and Iran meet,” said Bernhard Riegl, a professor at Nova Southeastern University who has studied the Persian Gulf for decades.
Sandy offshore islands and expansive seagrass beds make the Gulf an ideal habitat for nesting and breeding. Extensive mangrove forests and coral reef structures create protected nurseries for dolphins, seabirds, and whale sharks. Yet in springtime, traditionally the season for breeding, the war was raging.
A view of the vibrant underwater world at one of Musandam's popular diving sites in Khasab, Oman on August 28, 2025.
A satellite image shows oil in the Persian Gulf on April 2, 2026.
Only the hardiest marine life survives in these waters. Not only is the Persian Gulf one of the world’s hottest seas, but it also experiences sharp temperature changes throughout the year.
The water near Kuwait City, for example, goes from highs of around 36 degrees Celsius (97 Fahrenheit) in the height of summer to lows of around 16 degrees Celsius (60 Fahrenheit).
Razan Khalifa Al Mubarak, the UAE’s special envoy of the Minister of Foreign Affairs for Nature, called for an end to attacks on the Gulf in an editorial earlier this year.
“While the financial costs of damage can be calculated, the loss of ecosystems and species cannot. Once degraded, these systems may not recover,” she wrote.
The forecast is just the beginning. We’ll send you expert coverage and the stories behind the weather — so you always know more than just the number. Sign up for the newsletter
Contamination in the Gulf brings unique challenges as the body of water is relatively shallow — the deepest areas are only around 360 feet — and it is small, at 93,000 square miles. It also has only one entry and exit point connecting it to the open ocean: the Strait of Hormuz, which leads into the Gulf of Oman and the Indian Ocean beyond. This all means pollutants tend not to disperse or dilute easily.
No matter which country or vessel may be a target during this war, the repercussions will have wide impacts, Riegl said: “If we take a drop of oil and put it into the water on the Iranian side of the inflow into the Gulf, a year later it will be in Kuwait, two years later it will be on the Omani side of the Gulf and float out there.”
The 1991 Gulf War should be a cautionary tale, Riegl said. Retreating Iraqi forces set fire to hundreds of oil wells in Kuwait creating an environmental catastrophe felt throughout the Gulf.
An oil well fire at the Greater Burhan oil field in Kuwait, in August 9, 1991.
“The shorelines were devastatingly affected,” Riegl said. “In fact, in many areas of Saudi (Arabia), if you go and dig in the sand, there’s still old oil in there. It’s just very hard to get rid of.”
An additional concern is the fact that tens of millions of Gulf residents rely on desalination plants, which convert seawater into drinking water.
The six countries which form the Gulf Cooperation Council produce around a third of the world’s desalinated water. Bahrain and Kuwait are almost entirely dependent on desalination for all drinking water. Oil pollution in the Gulf could damage these facilities, imperiling water supply .
When hundreds of millions of gallons were intentionally released into the Persian Gulf during the Iraq war, Kuwait had to rely on Saudi Arabia, Turkey and others to provide hundreds of water tankers and trucks to deliver bottled water.
One perhaps unexpected repercussion of environmental damage in the Persian Gulf is that it could undermine scientists’ understanding of the impacts climate change will have on the world’s seas.
The Gulf’s high temperatures are roughly where marine tropics are expected to be around the year 2100, Riegl said.
A flock of birds near the shores of the Daymaniyat Islands Nature Reserve in Oman, on October 4, 2023.
The Gulf has “some of the most… valuable genetics of any sea on Earth, because a lot of the adaptation that will need to be done has already been achieved in the Gulf.”
“If we want to know what the ocean at large will look like at the end of the century,”he added, “the Gulf is the area to look at.”
CNN’s Kim Kelaita and Hiba Khurram contributed reporting.
