### 46. ★★★☆☆ Russian Offensive Campaign Assessment, October 22, 2026 - Institute for the Study of War

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T03:58:47+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Russian Offensive Campaign Assessment, October 22, 2026&nbsp;&nbsp;Institute for the Study of War

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Russian Offensive Campaign Assessment, October 22, 2026
The Kremlin's seeming interest in negotiations aims not to end the war in Ukraine but rather to secure economic concessions from the United States such that Russia can continue funding its war. Various Russian officials tried to present the October 21 meeting between US Special Representative to the Middle East Steve Witkoff and Russian President Vladimir Putin as evidence that Russia is willing to engage in meaningful negotiations to end the war while simultaneously attempting to extract economic concessions from the United States so that Russia can continue funding the war.[1] Russian Direct Investment Fund (RDIF) CEO Kirill Dmitriev, for example, claimed on October 21 that Witkoff and Putin used the October 21 meeting to agree to expand Russian-US economic cooperation and to “continue communicating.”[2] A Kremlin domestic policy official told Russian opposition source Verstka that the Kremlin does not expect negotiations to resolve the war in Ukraine but that Russian officials hope to extract economic concessions, which could boost Russia’s budget and give Russia a military advantage.[3]
Other Russian officials meanwhile demonstrated Russia’s unwillingness to make any meaningful concessions toward ending the war in Ukraine. Kremlin Spokesperson Dmitry Peskov stated on October 22 that Putin did not present any Russian peace proposal to Witkoff during their meeting and that Putin has not considered the composition of a potential Russian delegation for a future Russia-US-Ukraine trilateral meeting.[4] Russian State Duma deputies expressed skepticism on October 21 and 22 that negotiations could end the war and reiterated claims that freezing the current frontlines would be detrimental to Russia's own efforts to end the war.[5] A source close to the Russian Ministry of Foreign Affairs (MFA) told Verstka on October 22 that the Kremlin has been working for over 1.5 years to try to sway the Trump administration to Russia's side and that the Kremlin's current negotiation efforts are emphasizing Ukraine's alleged hopelessness. These are similar to the Kremlin's earlier negotiation tactics which tried to convince the United States and the West generally that Ukrainian defenses were going to collapse such that Ukraine should preemptively capitulate.[6]
The Kremlin is fundamentally interested only in buying time to resolve the war on the battlefield while Russia simultaneously reaps the benefits of economic cooperation with the United States. State Duma Defense Committee First Deputy Chairperson Alexei Zhuravlyov stated on October 21 that Russia must "press" Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky on the battlefield and force Ukraine to "capitulate."[7] Zhuravlyov added that Russia should not rely on diplomacy with the United States because Russian "successes on the ground” will strengthen Russia’s negotiating position.[8] Russian Presidential Aide Yuriy Ushakov similarly claimed that Russian forces maintain the "complete strategic initiative” and are conducting a "relentless offensive” in all key areas of the frontline.[9]
Russian forces are not winning on the battlefield, however, particularly as Ukrainian forces have stymied the planned Russian offensive to seize Donetsk Oblast. ISW has observed evidence that Russian forces suffered a net loss of 241 square kilometers of territory in Ukraine since September 1, 2026. The average rate of daily Russian advances has significantly declined since 2025, with an average of 0.25 square kilometers per day so far in 2026. Ukrainian forces have, comparatively, been contesting and seizing the operational initiative across several sectors of the frontline throughout 2026, including most recently in the Lyman direction with Ukraine's Operation Vivaldi which liberated over 270 square kilometers of territory and thwarted the Russian military command’s plan to encircle the Fortress Belt.[10] Russian forces are also facing mounting monthly casualties while the Russian recruitment rate continues to fall short of its loss rate.[11] Russian forces have suffered almost 37,000 casualties thus far in October 2026 — much higher than the 31,270 casualties for all of October 2025.[12] The Kremlin may face mounting challenges in the coming months, should Ukraine continue to contest the operational initiative and disrupt Russian offensive plans, potentially further pushing Russian commanders to urge Putin to conduct a mobilization in some form.[13]
The Kremlin is likely actively seeking economic cooperation with the United States as Russia faces significant financial strain. Sources familiar with the matter told Bloomberg on October 22 that even a few more months of continued war could exact lasting financial damage on Russian state-owned oil company Rosneft, as Ukrainian strikes against Rosneft's oil refineries have significantly reduced the company's profits and increased its debt.[14] Bloomberg added that repeated Ukrainian strikes have prevented Rosneft from completing repairs and restarting refinery operations and that strikes against complex secondary units that often include Western-sourced parts, like catalytic crackers, reformers, and hydrocrackers, are particularly difficult to repair. Russia remains highly susceptible to international sanctions that restrict its access to the technologically advanced components needed to repair the equipment damaged after Ukraine’s strikes.[15] The Kremlin is therefore pushing the United States for various forms of economic relief, so that Russia can continue funding its war against Ukraine.[16] The Kremlin has continuously sought to extract economic concessions during negotiations to alleviate the pressure on its oil industry — including restrictions on Ukrainian strikes against refineries and the relaxation of US sanctions against diesel exports — while expressing little real interest in ending the war.[17]
Russian forces conducted a massive missile and drone strike against Ukraine overnight on October 21 to 22 following the October 21 meeting between Witkoff and Putin — a continuation of Russia’s pattern of launching large strike packages in the days before and after major peace talks. The Ukrainian Air Force reported that Russian forces conducted a massive combined missile and drone strike primarily targeting Kyiv City and using an unspecified number of Iskander-M, S-400, Zircon, and Onyx missiles and 210 long-range drones, 110 of which were jet-powered.[18] Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky stated that Russian forces conducted three waves of ballistic missile strikes against Kyiv City using 21 missiles, damaging residential, energy, railway, and commercial infrastructure.[19] Zelensky stated that Russian strikes targeted 12 Ukrainian oblasts, killing at least two people and injuring at least 34 people. Russian forces regularly conduct massive strikes in the days right before and after bilateral and trilateral talks, such as the August 2025 US-Russia Alaska summit, the November 2025 US-Ukraine talks in Geneva, the November 2025 US-Russia talks in Moscow, the December 2025 US-Ukrainian-European and US-Russian talks, and the January-February 2026 US-Ukrainian-Russian talks in Abu Dhabi.[20]
Russian forces struck Kyiv City with cluster munitions to purposely harm the civilian population, likely as part of wider Russian efforts to distract from Ukraine’s recent battlefield successes and ultimately force Ukraine into capitulation by making daily life untenable. Zelensky stated that Russian forces struck Pechersk Raion, the historic center of Kyiv City, with three ballistic missiles with cluster warheads that have a dispersion diameter of 400 meters.[21] The strikes damaged monastery buildings and other civilian infrastructure near the Kyiv Pechersk Lavra and the Holy Presentation Monastery. Zelensky noted that there were no military targets in the area and that the objective of the strikes was to cause widespread destruction.[22] Kyiv City Mayor Vitaliy Klitschko stated that Russian forces also struck metro infrastructure on the Southern Bridge over the Dnipro River in Kyiv City and damaged six ambulances at an ambulance station in the city.[23] Ukrainian Presidential Advisor on Defense Technologies Serhiy “Flash” Beskrestnov stated that each Iskander-M ballistic missile’s cluster warhead releases 45 submunitions that detonate in the air and noted that the objective of the strikes was to destroy a portion of the historic center and injure as many people as possible.[24] Cluster munitions have a wide area of effect and are designed to kill enemy personnel or destroy light vehicles, underscoring Russia’s intent to maximize civilian harm and suffering. Cluster munitions historically have a high failure rate, and unexploded bomblets can cause lasting danger for civilians living in affected areas, further demonstrating Russia‘s aim to terrorize civilians.[25]
Russian strikes against Kyiv City are intended to make daily life untenable heading into Winter 2026-2027. Zelensky stated that Russian forces have struck more than 170 Ukrainian energy facilities since October 11 — the date when US President Donald Trump announced that Russia and Ukraine had agreed to an “energy ceasefire.”[26] The increasing intensity and brutality of Russia's long-range strike campaign ahead of Winter 2026-2027 highlights the urgency of providing Ukraine with sufficient air defense munitions, reliable low-latency satellite access over Russia, and other Western assistance that allows Ukraine to defend its skies from Russian missiles and drones.
The Kremlin's seeming interest in negotiations aims not to end the war in Ukraine but rather to secure economic concessions from the United States such that Russia can continue funding its war.
The Kremlin is fundamentally interested only in buying time to resolve the war on the battlefield while Russia simultaneously reaps the benefits of economic cooperation with the United States.
Russian forces are not winning on the battlefield, however, particularly as Ukrainian forces have stymied the planned Russian offensive to seize Donetsk Oblast.
The Kremlin is likely actively seeking economic cooperation with the United States as Russia faces significant financial strain.
Russian forces conducted a massive missile and drone strike against Ukraine overnight on October 21 to 22 following the October 21 meeting between Witkoff and Putin- a continuation of Russia’s pattern of launching large strike packages in the days before and after major peace talks.
Russian forces struck Kyiv City with cluster munitions to purposely harm the civilian population, likely as part of wider Russian efforts to distract from Ukraine’s recent battlefield successes and ultimately force Ukraine into capitulation by making daily life untenable.
Ukrainian forces recently advanced south of Vovchansk and in southern Kostyantynivka.
Ukrainian forces continued their long-range strike campaign against Russian military assets on October 21 and overnight on October 21 to 22.
We do not report in detail on Russian war crimes because these activities are well-covered in Western media and do not directly affect the military operations we are assessing and forecasting. We will continue to evaluate and report on the effects of these criminal activities on the Ukrainian military and the Ukrainian population and specifically on combat in Ukrainian urban areas. We utterly condemn Russian violations of the laws of armed conflict and the Geneva Conventions and crimes against humanity even though we do not describe them in these reports.
Ukrainian forces continued their long-range strike campaign against Russian military assets on October 21 and overnight on October 21 to 22. Ukrainian Center for Countering Disinformation Head Lieutenant Andriy Kovalenko reported on October 22 that Ukrainian forces struck the Russian 75th Arsenal of the Main Missile and Artillery Directorate (GRAU) air defense system storage and repair facility in northern Serpukhov, Moscow Oblast (roughly 371 kilometers from the international border), which reportedly repairs Russian air defense systems including the Buk-M1, Buk-M2, and S-300V systems.[27] Geolocated footage published on October 21 and 22 shows a large explosion at the Russian 75th Arsenal following a Ukrainian strike.[28]
Russian authorities continue to construct air defense structures at the Alabuga Special Economic Zone (SEZ) to defend drone production facilities from Ukrainian strikes. Radio Free Europe/Radio Liberty (RFE/RL) reported that satellite imagery collected on October 21 of drone production facilities at the Alabuga SEZ in the Republic of Tatarstan shows 33 newly constructed air defense positions, including concrete towers and new pads for Pantsir and Tor air defense systems.[29] Russian authorities had constructed two perimeters of lattice towers as part of passive drone defensive structures surrounding Geran drone production facilities at the Alabuga SEZ by Summer 2026.[30]
Russian objective: Create defensible buffer zones in Sumy Oblast along the international border
Several Russian milbloggers and a source reporting on the Russian Northern Grouping of Forces (GoF) claimed that Russian forces continued offensive operations in northern Sumy Oblast on October 21 and 22, but ISW has not observed evidence that Russian forces advanced.[31]
Ukrainian forces continued their intermediate-range strike campaign against Russian military assets in Bryansk Oblast. The Ukrainian General Staff reported on October 22 that Ukrainian forces struck a Russian electronic warfare station near Gulevka, Bryansk Oblast (roughly 32 kilometers from the international border) on October 21 or overnight on October 21 to 22.[32]
Russian objective: Push Ukrainian forces back from the international border to create a defensible buffer zone with Belgorod Oblast and approach Kharkiv City
Ukrainian forces recently advanced northeast of Kharkiv City. Russian forces struck Ukrainian positions south of Vovchansk (northeast of Kharkiv City), according to geolocated footage published on October 21, indicating that Ukrainian forces recently advanced in the area.[33]
Several Russian milbloggers claimed that Russian forces conducted offensive operations in the Velykyi Burluk direction on October 22, but ISW has not observed evidence that Russian forces advanced.[34]
Russian objective: Cross the Oskil River in Kharkiv Oblast and push westward into eastern Kharkiv Oblast and northern Donetsk Oblas t
The Ukrainian Khartyia Corps reported on October 21 that its Operation Veles is clearing a pipeline near Kupyansk that Russian forces use to infiltrate into Kupyansk, likely referring to the Trans-Balkan pipeline.[35] The success of Operation Veles may allow Ukrainian forces to clear Kupyansk of Russian infiltrators. The Khartiya Corps reported on October 22 that Ukrainian forces combined this underground offensive with ground operations and drone strikes, advancing more than 15 kilometers through the pipeline into the Russian near rear and potentially denying Russian forces the ability to conduct infiltration missions in the Kupyansk direction in the future.[36] The Khartiya Corps spokesperson reported that the pipeline runs two to four meters underground and is between one and 1.2 meters high.[37] The spokesperson stated that Russian servicemembers maintained positions in the tunnels for more than a month building barricades and awaiting further orders from the Russian military command.[38] Ukrainian Joint Forces Task Force Spokesperson Colonel Viktor Trehubov stated on October 22 that Russian forces used a pipeline, which travels north from Kupyansk and across the Oskil River through Holubivka (northeast of Kupyansk), to move covertly into Kupyansk and evacuate Russian troops and sometimes used civilians as human shields in these efforts.[39] Trehubov stated that the main objectives of the Ukrainian operation were to deny Russian forces the ability to covertly enter and exit the areas north of Kupyansk and across the Oskil River, as Ukrainian forces could previously only exert partial control over the pipeline’s exit. The Khartiya Corps reported that Russian forces lost over 100 personnel as a result of Operation Veles. The all-Russian pro-Ukrainian Freedom of Russia Legion (LSR) reported that it also participated in Operation Veles.[40]
Ukrainian forces' various efforts to eliminate Russian forces' use of the pipelines were unsuccessful such that Ukrainian forces have now turned to fighting underground. Russian forces have been using pipelines in the Kupyansk direction as their primary supply lines and infiltration routes since at least September 2025.[41] The Wall Street Journal reported on October 22 that Russian forces cut holes in tunnels and dug their way to the surface in order to launch drones against Ukrainian positions near Kupyansk.[42] Ukrainian forces previously struck these pipelines with drones, dropped munitions onto the pipelines with heavy bomber drones, and used unmanned ground vehicles (UGVs) to bring explosives to the area in order to disrupt Russian activity in the pipeline.[43] The Khartiya Corps spokesperson stated that Ukrainian strikes did not fully prevent Russian forces from using the pipelines, however, because Russian forces would dig into the soil and cut new entries into the tunnels, forcing Ukrainian personnel to themselves go underground to physically push Russians from the pipeline.[44]
Neither Russian nor Ukrainian sources reported ground activity in the Borova direction on October 22.
Russian Main Effort: Capture Ukraine's Fortress Belt and complete the seizure of Donetsk Oblast
Russian Subordinate Main Effort #1 – Encircle the Fortress Belt from the northwest
Russian objective: Seize Lyman and cross the Siverskyi Donets River to support a wide encirclement of the Fortress Belt – assessed objective of the Russian 25th Combined Arms Army and Western Grouping of Forces elements
The Russian military command appears to have deployed unmanned systems elements to the Lyman direction after Operation Vivaldi, likely to compensate for the withdrawal of the elite Rubikon Center for Advanced Unmanned Systems from the area. The commander of a Ukrainian regiment operating in the Lyman direction stated on October 22 that the Russian military command transferred elements of the elite Rubikon Center to the area during Ukraine's Operation Vivaldi in order to increase Russian reconnaissance and strike capabilities.[45] The commander reported that Ukrainian forces heavily targeted these drone operators, ultimately leading the Russian military command to withdraw Rubikon elements from the area. ISW has now observed reports that first-person view (FPV) drone operators of the Russian 50th Varyag Brigade (Unmanned Systems Forces [USF]) are striking Ukrainian forces in the area of responsibility (AoR) of the Russian 20th Combined Arms Army (CAA, Moscow Military District [MMD]) north of Lyman.[46] A Ukrainian OSINT account assessed that the presence of the 50th Varyag Brigade in this area indicates that elements of the brigade redeployed to the 20th CAA's AoR to stabilize the situation amid ongoing Ukrainian counterattacks.[47] The 50th Varyag Brigade traditionally conducts Geran-type drone strikes against the Ukrainian rear and more limited Geran-type and FPV drone strikes against frontline areas.[48] The presence of 50th Varyag Brigade FPV drone operators in the 20th CAA's AoR indicates that the Russian military command may be looking for alternate ways to stabilize the situation in the Lyman direction, particularly after the withdrawal of Rubikon elements.
Russian Subordinate Main Effort #2 – Advance on the Fortress Belt from the east
Russian objective: Advance on Slovyansk, Kramatorsk, and Druzhkivka from the east – assessed objective of the Russian 3rd Combined Arms Army and Southern Grouping of Forces elements
Russian forces continued limited offensive operations east of Slovyansk on October 21 and 22 but did not advance.[49]
Russian Subordinate Main Effort #3 – Advance on the Fortress Belt from the south
Russian objective: Advance on Kostyantynivka and Druzhkivka from the south and southwest, east of the Kazenyi Torets River – assessed objective of the Russian 8th Combined Arms Army and Southern Grouping of Forces elements
Ukrainian forces maintain positions in southern Kostyantynivka. Russian forces struck a Ukrainian position in southern Kostyantynivka, according to geolocated footage published on October 21, indicating that Ukrainian and Russian positions are intermixed in the area.[50]
Russian Subordinate Main Effort #4 – Encircle the Fortress Belt from the southwest
Russian objective: Conduct a wide encirclement of the Fortress Belt from near Dobropillya and Pokrovsk west of the Kazenyi Torets River – assessed objective of the Russian 51st Combined Arms Army and Central Grouping of Forces elements
Russian forces recently infiltrated southeast of Dobropillya. Ukrainian forces conducted an airstrike on a Russian-occupied building in southeastern Bilytske (southeast of Dobropillya), according to geolocated footage published on October 21, after what ISW assesses was a Russian infiltration mission.[51] Ukrainian military observer Kostyantyn Mashovets reported on October 21 that Russian forces infiltrated to the line between Bilytske and Svitle (both southeast of Dobropillya).[52]
Russian Supporting Effort: Advance into Dnipropetrovsk Oblast
Russian objective: Interdict Ukrainian ground lines of communication that support Ukrainian positions in Donetsk Oblast and envelop Ukrainian positions in southwestern Donetsk Oblast – assessed objective of elements of the Russian Central and Eastern groupings of forces
Russian forces conducted limited offensive operations in the Oleksandrivka and Novopavlivka directions on October 21 and 22 but did not advance.[53]
Russian objective: Maintain frontline positions, secure rear areas against Ukrainian strikes, and advance within tube artillery range of Zaporizhzhia City – assessed objective of the Russian Eastern and Dnepr Grouping of Forces
The Russian Ministry of Defense (MoD) continued to make aggrandized claims of Russian advances in Hulyaipole direction. The Russian MoD alleged on October 22 that Russian forces seized Myrivka (northwest of Hulyaipole, roughly 16 kilometers from ISW's furthest assessed Russian advances).[54] Several Russian milbloggers credited elements of the Russian 37th Motorized Rifle Brigade (36th Combined Arms Army [CAA], Eastern Military District [EMD]) and the 104th Guards Airborne (VDV)Regiment (76th Guards VDV Division) with the seizure of Myrivka.[55]
Ukrainian forces recently either advanced or maintained positions west of Orikhiv. Russian forces struck Ukrainian positions north of Mali Shcherbaky, according to geolocated footage published on October 21, indicating that Ukrainian forces maintain positions in the area contrary to Russian claims.[56]
Several Russian milbloggers expressed skepticism about the Russian MoD’s claimed seizure of Yulivka (northwest of Orikhiv) on October 21. [57] Russian milbloggers stated that the Russian MoD's October 21 claim about the seizure of Yulivka is “surprising” because Yulivka is 10 or more kilometers from the known line of contact.[58] Another Russian milblogger stated that the Russian MoD’s false claims about Yulivka are likely a deliberate exaggeration in order to deliberately misrepresent the battlefield situation to US negotiators.[59] ISW assesses that Yulivka is roughly 17.5 kilometers from the assessed frontline. A Russian milblogger credited 104th VDV Division with the alleged seizure of Yuliv.[60]
Ukrainian forces continued their intermediate-range strike campaign against Russian air defense systems in occupied Zaporizhia Oblast. The Ukrainian 7th Rapid Reaction Corps reported on October 22 that Ukrainian forces struck a Russian Buk-M1 air defense system and another unspecified Russian air defense system near occupied Dmytrivske, Zaporizhia Oblast (roughly 32 kilometers from the frontline).[61] The 7th Rapid Reaction Corps reported that it is becoming increasingly difficult for Russian forces to camouflage expensive equipment from drone operations, particularly as foliage decreases in the fall.
Russian forces launched an attack in the Dnipro direction on October 22 but did not advance.[62]
Russian Objective: Target Ukrainian military and civilian infrastructure in the rear and on the frontline
Note: The Ukrainian military ceased regularly reporting the number of Russian missiles fired and the number of missiles intercepted as of August 11.[63]
See topline text for further reporting on Russian missile and drone strikes against Ukraine
Ukraine's Main Military Intelligence Directorate (GUR) reported on October 22 that Russian forces began to use the new Filin cruise missile in late Summer 2026.[64] The GUR reported that the Filin cruise missile has a 3.5-meter-long carbon-fiber and fiberglass composite fuselage, a three-meter wingspan, and some 3D-printed parts and may have a maximum speed of 800 kilometers per hour and a range of up to 1,200 kilometers. The GUR reported that the Filin cruise missile comes in variants with 50- and 100-kilogram warheads (similar to the payload of a Geran-type drone) and launches from a ground launcher using a solid-propellent booster. The GUR noted that the Filin cruise missile uses a Chinese-made inertial navigation system and a 16-channel Kometa controlled reception pattern antenna (CPRA) for targeting and has a Russian R500 engine. The Russian development of the Filin cruise missile indicates Russia’s continued focus on producing scalable and more cost-effective ground- and air-launched cruise missiles that are difficult for Ukrainian forces to intercept at scale.
Russian efforts to increase its military presence in Belarus and further integrate Belarus into Russian-favorable frameworks
Russian Defense Minister Andrei Belousov met with Belarusian Defense Minister Lieutenant General Viktor Khrenin on October 22 to discuss deeper military cooperation.[65] Belousov and Khrenin met in Minsk, Belarus to discuss the coordination of military command bodies, cooperation between defense industries, joint air defense, and opportunities for the Russian and Belarusian militaries to conduct joint training. Belousov stated that both states will continue to work to create a unified defense and security state. Increased military cooperation between the Russian and Belarusian militaries is part of Russia’s efforts to maintain its de facto annexation of Belarus, as ISW has long assessed.[66]
Note: ISW does not receive any classified material from any source, uses only publicly available information, and draws extensively on Russian, Ukrainian, and Western reporting and social media as well as commercially available satellite imagery and other geospatial data as the basis for these reports. References to all sources used are provided in the endnotes of each update.

### 48. ★★★☆☆ Markets tumble as concerns about Iran war and high AI spending collide - NBC News

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-22T20:12:29+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Markets tumble as concerns about Iran war and high AI spending collide&nbsp;&nbsp;NBC News

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The U.S. and Israel are reportedly preparing to resume military strikes on Iran following the Nov. 3 midterm elections. Atta Kenare / AFP via Getty
Oct. 22, 2026, 11:09 AM EDT  /  Updated   Oct. 22, 2026, 4:12 PM EDT
U.S. equity markets sold off sharply Thursday, as fears that Israel and the U.S. would restart attacks against Iran sent oil prices higher and major tech stocks failed to hold on to recent gains.
The S&P 500 closed down 1.2%, while the tech-heavy Nasdaq dropped 2.6%. The price of international Brent crude climbed nearly 4% to $113 a barrel, its highest level since May, while U.S. crude gained more than 2% to close at more than $99 a barrel.
Demand for Treasury bonds also increased — bucking the trend of the past several weeks — as investors sought out the safe-haven assets amid riskier ones like tech stocks selling off. The yield on the 10-year Treasury note fell below 5% for the first time since late September.
The market moves were sparked in part by reports that Israel and the U.S. were preparing for a military operation in Iran after the November midterm elections.
Likely targets include Iran’s energy infrastructure and nuclear sites, according to The New York Times .
U.S. military and diplomatic personnel were also being evacuated from several countries in the Middle East, NBC News reported.
Iranian officials have promised widespread retaliation to any strikes, pointing to critical Middle East data center sites and undersea internet cables as potential targets.
“It’s been another difficult 24 hours for markets, as investors grapple with AI growth concerns, higher oil prices, and ongoing fiscal stress,” Henry Allen, director and macro strategist at Deutsche Bank, wrote in a note to clients Thursday.
Major tech stocks led the declines in equity markets, with Meta and Tesla dropping after both companies reported quarterly earnings Wednesday.
Meta, led by Mark Zuckerberg, reported that a scarcity of computing power is hindering the expansion of its Muse personal assistant overseas. Annabelle Gordon / Bloomberg via Getty Meta’s earnings and revenues beat forecasts, but only after excluding billions of dollars in legal penalties the company paid last quarter. It also warned investors that it expects to increase its spending in the coming year to meet demands for computing power.
A separate regulatory filing revealed that Meta intends to spend an additional $825 billion on contracts related to its AI infrastructure build-out.
Tesla, meanwhile, said it was reining in its capital spending plans, disappointing investors who were hoping for a signal of more aggressive expansion.
Elon Musk&#x27;s Tesla reported it was scaling up its Cybercab fleet in Austin, Texas. Win McNamee / Getty The slide in the Nasdaq got additional fuel Thursday after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang said in an appearance on CNBC that capital to finance the AI investment boom was becoming “scarce” due to higher interest rates and a glut of existing spending commitments.
Despite Thursday’s rout, the S&P 500 remains near the all-time highs it set earlier this month.

A major sell-off in French government debt has also dragged European stocks lower in recent weeks.
But a significant market drawdown in the U.S. threatens to further dent Republicans’ prospects in the upcoming midterm elections.
President Donald Trump frequently points to solid stock market gains as evidence that his economic agenda has made Americans richer.
A prolonged market stumble ahead of the Nov. 3 elections would undermine this core argument for Trump and the GOP.

### 49. ★★★☆☆ EU leaders to press Burnham to pay more towards Ukraine’s defence - The Guardian

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN Geopolitics
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T06:37:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: EU leaders to press Burnham to pay more towards Ukraine’s defence&nbsp;&nbsp;The Guardian

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Rescue workers put out a fire at a residential building destroyed by a Russian missile strike on Kyiv, Ukraine, on Wednesday. Photograph: Evgeniy Maloletka/AP
Rescue workers put out a fire at a residential building destroyed by a Russian missile strike on Kyiv, Ukraine, on Wednesday. Photograph: Evgeniy Maloletka/AP
EU leaders to press Burnham to pay more towards Ukraine’s defence
Officials will call on UK to ‘step up’ financial support at Brussels summit – with post-Brexit issues also on agenda
EU leaders will press Andy Burnham at a summit in Brussels to contribute more to Ukraine’s defence effort in 2027.
EU officials argue that the UK, Canada and Japan should do more to support Ukraine , but entreaties to these G7 countries for extra financial aid at recent international meetings have not been answered, Brussels says.
The European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen , will call on the Canadian prime minister, Mark Carney, to contribute to Ukraine’s looming financial shortfall at an EU-Canada summit in Montreal next week.
A similar request to the British prime minister will be made when he meets von der Leyen and the European Council president, António Costa, for a long-awaited EU-UK summit on 20 November in Brussels. It comes as the UK chancellor, John Healey, prepares for his first budget next week, weighing numerous calls for more spending against the rising cost of government borrowing and a darkening economic outlook.
Healey said earlier this month he was “working on different ways we can step up support for Ukraine – military, diplomatic and economic”.
Firefighters tackle a fire in a residential building after a Russian drone strike on Kramatorsk on Thursday. Photograph: Diego Herrera Carcedo/Anadolu/Getty Images Ukraine says it faces a €70bn (£58bn) funding shortfall in 2027 to pay for its defence and civilian needs.
The EU and its member states are paying for 80% of Ukraine’s financial needs, according to the commission, which considers a “fair” EU share to be two-thirds, with the remaining third covered by other allies. “We will continue to lead, but we urgently need other partners to step up,” von der Leyen told reporters last week, without naming countries.
The EU last year agreed a €90bn loan for Ukraine, intended to cover two-thirds of Kyiv’s funding in 2026 and 2027. But as Russia escalates its attacks, dragging the war into its fifth winter, Ukraine’s costs continue to spiral.
EU officials have prioritised non-EU G7 countries, above all the UK, Canada and Japan. But they are now casting the net wider and may look to Switzerland, South Korea, Australia and New Zealand to promise additional aid to Ukraine.
The British government says it has committed £25bn to Ukraine, including £16bn in military aid.
The UK is Ukraine’s fourth largest donor, behind the US, Germany and France, according to the Kiel Institute’s Ukraine Support Tracker . But the picture changes when the UK’s share is weighted to GDP. The UK was assessed by the Kiel researchers as spending 0.86% of national income to support Ukraine, compared with 1.1% from France and 1.5% from Germany. These three large European economies spent significantly less than the Nordics and Baltics, such as Denmark (4.2%) and Estonia (3.8%). The UK is more in line with France and Germany when their contribution to EU programmes is stripped out.
The European Commission declined to comment on specific countries, but referred to a recent statement: “We always said that we cannot walk this alone … therefore our partners in general must step up.”
A Kyiv shopping mall damaged by a Russian missile strike on Thursday. Photograph: Sofiia Lavska/Apostrophe/Global Images Ukraine/Getty Images Ukraine will be one of several issues on the agenda at the EU-UK summit, where leaders are expected to conclude three agreements: a food and animal health (SPS) deal to reduce Brexit paperwork; the linkage of the EU and UK emissions trading schemes (ETS) to protect British industry from European carbon taxes; and a youth mobility scheme to enable 18- to 30-year-olds to live, work, travel and study across both sides of the Channel.
In a positive sign for British negotiators, the European Commission proposed deferring the question of university tuition fees, one of the last big sticking points. The UK would like to charge EU students at British universities the international rate, about four times higher than the domestic fee. The EU, where many officials are alumni of British universities, wants to return to the pre-Brexit status quo, when EU students paid home rates.
Commission officials have proposed resolving this thorny question in technical talks within six months of the 20 November summit, rather than risk derailing the high-profile leaders’ meeting. But senior EU sources are confident they still have leverage on the issue, as the SPS and ETS deals will not come into force until there is a youth mobility agreement acceptable to the European side, including on tuition fees.
“The three elements are a sort of gift to the UK, but not a massive gift,” an EU diplomat said, adding that the EU would still have clout in the negotiations over the fees. “The leverage is that the UK needs this [the other agreements].” UK hopes to strike economic security agreements with the EU , to avoid British industry becoming collateral damage of Europe’s defensive measures against China, are seen as another source of leverage.
But a second diplomat said there was no consensus among the EU’s 27 member states on postponing the tuition fees question until after the summit: “There is no clear majority saying ‘let’s drop it now for six months’,” they said.
Youth mobility scheme may be delayed to ensure success of UK-EU summit

Share ‘Life will not be easy for Europe this year,’ IEA chief warns on social media
The International Energy Agency chief Fatih Birol poses during an interview with Reuters in Paris, France, December 15, 2023 [Sarah Meyssonnier/Reuters]
Berlin, Germany – Europe is entering winter with historically low gas reserves and cannot withstand further supply shocks, Fatih Birol, head of the International Energy Agency, has warned, as the US-Israel war on Iran disrupts global supply chains.
Speaking to Al Jazeera on the sidelines of an international conference in Berlin on Wednesday, Birol said all regions are being hit by energy disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz but that Europe is particularly vulnerable and will likely face rising costs of liquefied natural gas (LNG) in the coming months – even if peace returns to the Gulf today.
list of 4 items list 1 of 4 Qatar warns global energy shockwaves from Iran war have yet to peak
list 2 of 4 US leaving oil-for-security approach, not Middle East: Envoy Tom Barrack
list 3 of 4 As Macron visits Gulf, can France forge its own energy-for-security future?
list 4 of 4 EU revises Arctic strategy as pressure from Russia, US grows
“The best day for a ceasefire would have been yesterday,” the energy economist, whose agency advises wealthy member nations and coordinates emergency responses, said.
“Normally before the winter, European countries have a lot of gas stocks, but the gas stocks we have while we are entering this winter are much lower than historical averages,” he said, adding that Europe’s gas is competing with that of Asian countries, which are even more dependent on the Middle East.
“I think if we have a harsh winter, we may see higher prices of natural gas in Europe,” Birol said, adding that the increasingly political nature of energy adds to the uncertainty. “Life will not be easy for Europe this year and also next year.”
European nations have long relied on imported gas to generate electricity for industries and warm homes in the freezing winter. Nearly a 10th of the continent’s LNG transited through the Hormuz Strait before the war. However, Iran’s attacks on vessels have plummeted imports.
As the winter chill settles, the European Union’s natural gas storage is only 72 percent full, down from the usual 90 percent. The shortfall is equivalent to the energy needed to power 10 to 12 million homes, analysts say.
EU energy ministers are now mulling over whether they should ask households and businesses to ration consumption, according to news reports.
Birol said he had long thought about the dangers of relying on the Strait of Hormuz. In fact, he said, his standard question to recruits seeking to join his agency was often: What would they do if the strait shuts down today?
The fallout has hardly come as a surprise. The flare-ups in the alternative Bab al-Mandeb strait, he added, have only worsened things.
“World leaders, how do they think?” Birol, who was born in Turkiye, said in his usual measured tone.
“I am really disappointed that governments didn’t take the necessary measures. Now, everybody is in a hurry, taking measures; the Middle East countries are building new pipelines, expanding the new pipelines that are bypassing the Strait of Hormuz … but in my view, these efforts should have been done much earlier.”
The EU, whose ageing refineries have processed less crude in recent years, also faces shortages of imported refined products such as the diesel that powers the bloc’s trucking, agricultural and industrial sectors.
Birol said Ukraine’s attacks on Russian refineries were also behind a looming diesel scarcity. About a third of Russian deliveries have stopped coming, he said.
When the Group of Seven (G7) nations agreed to push 400 million barrels of crude and diesel from their strategic reserves in March, it was Birol who coordinated how those emergency stocks reached global markets – the third time he has had to oversee emergency stock releases since Russia’s invasion of Ukraine in 2022.
For years, the energy economist said, his agency warned that the bloc was too tethered to Russia’s fuel, and that it had turned away too early from nuclear-generated power, which gave countries domestic leverage.
When Russia invaded Ukraine, his warnings manifested after Moscow cut gas supplies in retaliation for sanctions. The EU plans to permanently phase out Russian gas, but amid the Gulf crisis, members like France have again stepped up orders.
Industry experts say emergency reserves that hit the market have limited use, as some estimate only a few months will be covered.
But Birol argued that his agency recorded real impact – a $20 drop in soaring oil prices immediately after the G7 countries agreed to release 100 billion barrels of crude oil and diesel earlier this month.
Prices are, however, on the rise again. Gas has surged by 140 percent since February, and Brent crude is trading well above $100.
Whatever happens now, the war will be felt for months, Birol pointed out, adding that it has irrevocably altered how energy will travel around the world.
It is also altering Europe’s politics, Birol noted. Russia has wielded gas as a weapon. And populist parties, from France to Germany, are capturing growing public anger to frame mainstream governments as incompetent.
“I have never ever seen the dark and long shadow of geopolitics so dominant in the energy sector,” Birol mused. “The world has gone through so many difficult periods, but this is the worst in terms of the geopolitics dominating the energy game. Energy is not currently considered a business but a political leverage. As an energy man, this worries me.”
The EU still has about 80 percent of its emergency stocks, and Birol hopes he will not have to oversee a fourth release. But the world’s supply can’t take more shocks, he said.
“I really hope that both of the straits will be unconditionally opened sometime soon; otherwise, in the energy world, we don’t have much more buffer zones to still keep the prices at a certain level (as) we have today,” he concluded.

Oil prices climbed amid reports of potential fresh U.S. and Israeli strikes on Iran, with Brent crude topping $114 a barrel.
Meta, Nvidia and AMD recovered in premarket trading after Thursday's sell-off, while CoreWeave and SK Hynix also advanced.
Moderna extended losses after plunging nearly 18% on Thursday, while Western Digital fell despite beating earnings expectations and issuing an upbeat outlook.
U.S. stock futures were lower early on Friday after a sharp sell-off in chip stocks, as concerns grew that the artificial intelligence trade could lose near-term momentum.
As of 6 a.m. ET, Nasdaq 100 futures edged 0.04% lower, while Dow futures fell 0.60%. S&P 500 futures tumbled 0.23%.

Earlier, on Thursday, the benchmark indexes ended mixed, with the S&P 500 down 1.2%, the Nasdaq 100 falling 2.9%, and the Dow Jones Industrial Average remaining flat.
Retail sentiment on Stocktwits remained weak. Sentiment toward the SPDR S&P 500 ETF (SPY), which tracks the S&P 500, trended in 'extremely bearish' territory over the past day. The Invesco QQQ Trust (QQQ), which tracks the Nasdaq 100, saw sentiment shift to 'bearish' from 'extremely bearish', while sentiment towards the SPDR Dow Jones Industrial Average ETF Trust (DIA) remained in the 'extremely bearish' zone.
Oil prices rose again amid escalating geopolitical tensions, with reports suggesting that the U.S. and Israel are considering fresh strikes on Iran after the U.S. midterm elections in November. Tehran has vowed to retaliate against any new attacks, potentially targeting economic and civilian infrastructure, the Wall Street Journal reported.
As of 6 a.m. ET, Brent crude futures expiring December were up 1.1% to $114 a barrel, while U.S. West Texas Intermediate crude futures gained 1.6% % to over $101 per barrel.
Meta Platforms (META): Shares dipped 0.6% in early premarket trading on Friday, recovering slightly after falling more than 7% on Thursday, when investors reacted negatively to another increase in the company's annual AI spending forecast and plans to issue new shares to help fund the investment. The sell-off came despite better-than-expected results and a strong forecast, highlighting investor concerns about the scale of AI investments and the prospect of even cash-rich technology companies taking on debt to finance them.
Nvidia (NVDA): The stock edged 0.4% higher in early premarket trading on Friday after ending Thursday's regular session 4.4% down. Adding to concerns over Meta's rising AI spending, Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang told CNBC that "there is a scarcity of capital" for AI infrastructure, adding that "the demand for capital is much greater than the availability."
Advanced Micro Devices (AMD): Shares gained nearly 1% in early premarket trading on Friday, recovering some ground after ending Thursday's session more than 8% lower. Like Meta Platforms and Nvidia, AMD was attempting a rebound after a sharp sell-off in AI-related stocks in the previous session.
CoreWeave (CRWV): The stock was up 2% in premarket trading on Friday after hitting a fresh 17-month low of $53.50 during Thursday's session. Concerns over mounting debt and broader weakness in the AI sector have weighed on the stock. Despite the premarket gains, CRWV was on track for a third straight week of losses as rising borrowing costs and concerns about slowing AI demand continued to weigh on sentiment.
SK Hynix (SKHY): Shares rose almost 5% in premarket trading on Friday. The stock closed 4.4% higher on Thursday, reversing earlier losses of around 3% after the chipmaker halted its review of potential pre-IPO fundraising and listing options for its U.S. subsidiary, Solidigm. According to CNBC, Josh Gilbert, lead analyst for APAC at investment platform eToro, said the recovery suggested concerns over a potential Solidigm IPO had weighed on investor sentiment.
Moderna Inc. (MRNA): Shares fell over 4% in premarket trading on Friday, after Moderna's cancer-vaccine rally hit a setback on Thursday. MRNA is heading for its worst weekly performance in three months, while Mizuho analyst Jared Holz warned that extreme volatility had made the stock a "do-not-trade" for many investors. On Thursday, the shares plunged nearly 18% to close at $202.63, marking their steepest single-day decline in more than two months.
Western Digital Corp. (WDC): The stock was down around 2% in premarket trading despite the company beating fiscal first-quarter earnings expectations. The hard-drive maker delivered stronger profits and an upbeat second-quarter outlook, addressing concerns that the storage boom was losing momentum. However, the upbeat results failed to lift sentiment.
Bloom Energy Corp. (BE): Bloom Energy Corp. (BE): Shares of the fuel cell maker dropped as much as 2% in premarket trading on Friday after plunging nearly 9% on Thursday and extending losses overnight. The sell-off followed its third-quarter results, which raised concerns over capital expenditure and cash flow.
Other tickers trending on Stocktwits at the time of writing included Nokia (NOK), Walmart (WMT) and Charles Schwab (SCHW).
On the economic front, traders will watch for the final University of Michigan consumer sentiment reading.
Meanwhile, American Express Co. (AXP) and Schlumberger Ltd. (SLB) are among the companies scheduled to report earnings before the opening bell.

Major chip stocks, including Nvidia Corp. (NVDA), Micron Technology Inc. (MU) and Advanced Micro Devices Inc. (AMD) ended the session in red.
Chip-related ETFs also declined at Thursday's close. The iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX) fell nearly 7% at close and continued to decline overnight. The Direxion Daily Semiconductor Bull 3X Shares (SOXL) plunged more than 20%, while VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) fell nearly 6% at close.

Amid stalled negotiations with Tehran, media reports indicate that the U.S. and Israel are planning potential attacks on Iran in the coming weeks.

He also said that while Washington could end the war immediately, it was working to ensure a lasting outcome.
Iran's Major General Mohsen Rezaei has reportedly said, "Negotiations have been halted, and if a war breaks out, undersea internet cables, data centers, and AI facilities could be among Iran's targets."

### 56. ★★★☆☆ If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, History Says Investors Who Do This 1 Thing Always Win Out - The Motley Fool

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-22T16:00:00+00:00
- **发生了什么**: If a Stock Market Crash Is Coming, History Says Investors Who Do This 1 Thing Always Win Out&nbsp;&nbsp;The Motley Fool

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The stock market has been shaky lately, with the S&P 500 ( ^GSPC -1.21% ) , Nasdaq Composite ( ^IXIC -2.60% ) , and Dow Jones Industrial Average ( ^DJI +0.02% ) all slipping from their all-time highs earlier this year.
Investors are feeling the uncertainty, too. The Fear and Greed Index measures investor sentiment on a scale from 0 to 100, with lower numbers indicating greater fear. In early May, the index peaked at 71 -- firmly in the "greed" category. As of this writing, it sits at 28, indicating fearful investor sentiment.
In other words, if you&#x27;re feeling nervous about the market right now, you&#x27;re in good company. Fortunately, history says one simple, proven strategy can help protect your investments right now.
If you&#x27;re worried about a market crash, it can be tempting to sell your investments now before prices plunge. While that strategy may sound safer, it can be costly.
The market&#x27;s short-term performance will always be unpredictable to an extent. When the market stumbles, it&#x27;s impossible to know whether it&#x27;s just a short-term fluctuation or the beginning of a crash.
For example, earlier this year, the S&P 500 plunged amid fears that the war in Iran would drag the market into a recession. Almost immediately, though, it rebounded. As of this writing, the index is up more than 13% so far this year.
While it may sound counterintuitive, history shows that the single best way to avoid losing money during a downturn is to continue investing consistently. When stock prices plunge, your portfolio may lose value . But you won&#x27;t actually lock in losses unless you sell your investments for less than you paid for them.
For example, say you sold your stocks in March of this year, expecting the market to fall further. In that case, you&#x27;d have been hit twice: first by selling at discounted prices, and second by having to pay higher prices to reinvest later after the market rebounded.
Timing the market accurately is nearly impossible, and it can often cost you more than you gain.
The market has a flawless track record of long-term growth
Decades&#x27; worth of history proves that staying invested for the long term is the most lucrative way to build wealth in the stock market -- even when the short term is volatile.
During the dot-com bubble burst in the early 2000s, for instance, the S&P 500 sank by nearly 50% as the tech sector imploded. It took years for the index to reach a new all-time high, and shortly after it did, the Great Recession began in 2007 -- becoming the most severe economic downturn post-WWII.
Despite all of this volatility, however, the S&P 500 has soared by more than 750% since January 2000. In other words, if you&#x27;d invested $10,000 in an S&P 500 index fund or ETF back then and didn&#x27;t contribute another dollar, you&#x27;d have more than $85,000 by today.
Along with a long-term outlook, it&#x27;s equally important to invest in quality stocks. Not all companies will recover from a market crash or recession, especially if they&#x27;re built on shaky foundations. During the dot-com bubble, for instance, many tech stocks surged despite weak finances or unsustainable business models. When the bubble popped, those stocks fared the worst.
No matter what may come this year or beyond, healthy stocks are best positioned for long-term growth. The right investments can help avoid losses during a downturn while setting you up for lucrative gains, too.

### 57. ★★★☆☆ Defense giant Saab sees orders triple after Gripen fighter jet deal with Ukraine

- **分类**: 🇪🇺 欧洲
- **来源**: CNBC Top
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T08:10:27+00:00
- **发生了什么**: European governments race to turn defense spending pledges into military capability, boosting companies' order books.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Saab Q3 earnings: Fighter jet maker beats expectations as orders triple
Swedish defense giant Saab on Friday reported better-than-expected third-quarter earnings and sales, while orders trippled.
New orders came in at 65.8 billion Swedish kronor, up from 20.9 billion kronor a year earlier.
"The growing customer demand is reflected in major contract awards, including Gripen for Ukraine, GlobalEye and an order related to Germany&#x27;s new frigates," CEO Micael Johansson said.
Swedish defense giant Saab reported better-than-expected third-quarter earnings and revenue on Friday, as new orders more than tripled from a year earlier, boosted by a deal to supply Gripen fighter jets to Ukraine.
New orders came in at 65.8 billion Swedish kronor ($6.6 billion), up from 20.9 billion kronor a year earlier, beating FactSet expectations of 56.4 billion kronor.
Third-quarter sales reached 20.6 billion kronor, ahead of FactSet expectations of 20 billion kronor. Earnings before interest and taxes (EBIT) came in at 1.95 billion kronor, also beating forecasts of 1.9 billion kronor.
Stockholm-listed shares rose about 2% shortly after market open, amid a mixed defense sector. The Stoxx 600 index was down 0.4%.
The results come as European governments race to turn defense spending pledges into military capability.
Saab&#x27;s order intake included a 24.6 billion kronor contract for 16 Gripen E fighter jets with the Swedish Defence Materiel Administration for Ukraine, signed in June and booked in the third quarter. Deliveries are scheduled for 2029-2030.
"The growing customer demand is reflected in major contract awards, including Gripen for Ukraine, GlobalEye and an order related to Germany&#x27;s new frigates," CEO Micael Johansson said in a statement.
Orders booked in the quarter also include a 10.1 billion kronor order for its military surveillance aircraft GlobalEye to an undisclosed customer in the Middle East, and an 8.7 billion kronor order to equip warships for the German Navy.
Saab, whose products range from Gripen fighter jets and submarines to missiles and advanced electronics, has seen exponential order growth since Russia&#x27;s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in early 2022.
Even with an ever-growing order book , booming sales, and increasing profitability, Saab stock, like many of its peers, has taken a hit in recent months as some investors question whether valuations have run ahead of the industry&#x27;s ability to deliver.
Rising geopolitical tensions, including recent airspace violations, drone incidents and ongoing conflicts, are driving demand for defense capabilities, Johansson said. He added that Saab is expanding production capacity and investments to help it meet growing customer needs.
"The long-term market outlook remains favourable," he said, as Saab reported that the order growth was driven by a more than sixfold surge in large orders to 52.1 billion kronor, while small orders rose 84% and medium-sized orders fell 16%.
The total order backlog amounted to 364.2 billion at the end of September, up from 274.5 billion at the beginning of the year, marking a sixth consecutive quarter of order book growth.
Saab CEO: Europe needs joint defense procurement and industry partnerships
In July, Saab said it was looking to boost its production of jets from 15 per year to between 20 and 30.
"We can go further than that if we now get sort of partners involved in a more substantial way," Johansson told CNBC at the time.
Johansson also emphasized that European governments must fundamentally rethink how they buy weapons, moving away from fragmented national procurement toward long-term partnerships with industry.

### 58. ★★★☆☆ Hospitals deliberately targeted by Sudan's RSF fighters, BBC finds

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-23T10:23:07+00:00
- **发生了什么**: The UN human rights chief Volker Türk says the multiple attacks may amount to war crimes.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Figure caption, Watch: Doctors told the BBC about the targeting of Sudanese hospitals
By Barbara Plett Usher ,Â  Africa correspondent Â  and Â  Mohamed Madi
A BBC investigation has found evidence that Sudanese paramilitary fighters systematically destroyed el-Fasher&#x27;s healthcare system during their long siege and subsequent takeover of the city in Darfur a year ago.
UN human rights chief Volker TÃ¼rk called the findings "deeply disturbing", saying multiple attacks by the Rapid Support Forces (RSF) on civilian infrastructure, including health facilities, were serious violations of international humanitarian law and may amount to war crimes.
The health minister of the group&#x27;s civilian-affiliated government dismissed the allegations as propaganda.
Dr Alaa Eldin Nugud said it was a deliberate attempt by the national army and its allies to smear the RSF&#x27;s reputation.
Using sources who were on the ground in el-Fasher, the BBC has found details of at least 43 separate attacks on hospitals and healthcare sites from May 2024 to October 2025 - some hit multiple times.
Under international law it is illegal to attack health facilities or their staff during conflict.
The investigation also reveals the toll on medical workers in the western city. In the period from April 2023 to October 2025, the BBC&#x27;s data shows 120 were injured, 55 killed and 10 forcibly "disappeared" or abducted.
"The RSF [were] trying to evacuate the hospitals, to put them out of service because the health system is the cornerstone of any city or any state," says Dr Muhamed Suliman, a surgeon who was based in el-Fasher during the siege and spoke to the BBC.
"The RSF started hitting these areas so citizens have no longer any way to stay there. It was intense. It was deliberate. It was planned."
The investigation comes a year after the fall of el-Fasher, one of the most brutal chapters of the civil war which erupted in April 2023 between the RSF and Sudan&#x27;s military.
Both sides have been accused of attacks on health facilities.
Image caption, El-Fasher residents had little access to healthcare during the long siege of the city
In March 2026, the World Health Organization (WHO) condemned the bombing of al-Daein hospital in RSF-held eastern Darfur, which killed dozens and forced the site to close. Sudan&#x27;s military is believed to have carried out the drone strikes, although it has denied responsibility.
But events in el-Fasher represent "one of the clearest and most severe examples of the wider impact of the conflict on health care in Sudan," the WHO said in response to a BBC query.
UN investigators have reported that the fighting in el-Fasher bore the "hallmarks of genocide", where historical ethnic animosities have seen Arab militias - now the backbone of RSF forces - target non-Arab groups such as those that populate the city.
The BBC&#x27;s investigation reveals for the first time a detailed chronology of attacks on healthcare during the long siege of the city.
Reports of mass killings in Sudan have echoes of its dark past
Sudan army drones hit mourners and fuel market killing more than 300 people, sources say
Some medical facilities in el-Fasher closed down within weeks of the country&#x27;s civil war starting in April 2023.
Crucially this included one of the main hospitals - the Teaching Hospital - located in a conflict zone in the middle of the city.
Its operations were moved to el-Fasher&#x27;s South Hospital. There medical services continued, although increasingly hindered by power cuts and dwindling supplies.
The situation changed dramatically a year later, when the RSF mounted its siege of el-Fasher.
In late May and early June of 2024, the South Hospital was shelled several times as paramilitary fighters pushed forward from their positions in the east of the city.
The first bombing came as a shock, says Dr Suliman.
The area around the hospital that was hit was "very crowded because it was the only working hospital in the city providing surgical services", he says.
On that day medical staff operated on 16 injured patients, he added.
The attacks culminated in an RSF raid on 8 June. Hospital staff were taken by surprise - many of them escaped over the back wall as the RSF fighters entered the building, says Dr Mustafa Ahmed, a pharmaceutical specialist.
But the director general of the hospital, surgeon Izzeldin Ahmed Dawaud Asow, encountered a group of the paramilitaries outside his office.
They did not know he was a doctor, he said, but denounced him with ethnic slurs.
"There are no citizens here. You are Falangi," they said, using a derogatory term implying that non-Arab Sudanese are foreigners.
Dr Asow says he was beaten with rifle butts and lost consciousness at one point. Ultimately, he saved himself by pretending to be dead.
"They put their hand on my chest, and I stopped breathing. And they said: &#x27;Leave him, leave him. The rubbish has died. Leave him.&#x27; They left me."
That same day the RSF was driven out by the Sudanese military and its allies and Dr Asow returned to find the bodies of several patients and the surgical equipment in his operating room thoroughly destroyed, he said.
The hospital closed and later fell into RSF hands when the army retreated.
Dr Nugud, a prominent Sudanese surgeon who has joined the RSF-backed civilian government as health minister, insisted the South Hospital was being used as a military base.
According to international law, hospitals can lose their protection if they are used by a party to the conflict to commit an "act harmful to the enemy".
In an interview with the BBC, Dr Nugud declared that missile launchers were set up on the grounds and shared a video showing remains of weapons filmed more than a year after the hospital closed, which he said supported his claim.
He also showed us what he called maps of landmines planted around the Teaching Hospital, which is located next to Sudanese military headquarters.
Doctors told us the hospital was not incorporated into the base and said they visited it to pick up medical equipment during the war.
The BBC has found no independent evidence of Dr Nugud&#x27;s allegations. And the government of the Darfur region categorically rejected the charges.
After the South Hospital shut, its staff and remaining equipment were moved to the Saudi Maternity Hospital in the west of the city, where the army had its main bases.
Within days the pace of attacks on the facility picked up. At this stage the medical charity MÃ©decins Sans FrontiÃ¨res (MSF) sounded the alarm.
"We do not know if hospitals are being intentionally targeted, butâ¦ the belligerents are not taking any precautions to spare them," it said in a statement.
However, in the course of our investigation we spoke to six doctors who described a pattern of systematic targeting.
They told us how Saudi Hospital was repeatedly struck - on some days up to 25 times, with a mix of shelling and drones, the latter "intelligent rockets" with precision-guided attacks.
Smaller medical facilities also came under fire. The BBC&#x27;s timeline records the steady closure of private hospitals and neighbourhood clinics.
Furthermore, when some of these healthcare buildings shifted locations, the alternate venues were also attacked.
The Specialised Children&#x27;s Hospital, which shut down at the beginning of the war along with the Teaching Hospital, moved to two other locations, both of which were then shelled and forced to close.
Similarly, the main dialysis centre was struck and then attacked again after it relocated.
By the time the RSF seized the city in October 2025, the Saudi Hospital was the only functioning health facility left.
The medical staff there had rallied to try to keep working as conditions worsened.
Doctors described how sandbags were set up to protect the building.
They built underground bunkers out of steel containers and established a number of alternative sites to house and receive patients and conduct surgery as the threat of attacks and the damage to the main structure steadily increased.
Dr Asow said this makeshift surgery was attacked five times in 2025.
In one case, he says, he was operating on a patient&#x27;s neck when the building was shaken by an explosion - the medical staff ran out in fear, but he stayed.
In another case, health workers had prepared the theatre for an operation, but he told them to move the patient elsewhere because it needed a deep clean given the relentless case load. Shortly afterward the room was destroyed by shells - and the patient survived.
"From that day, some of my staff called me &#x27;Mr Deep Cleaning&#x27; because they think that the issue of deep cleaning saved that patient," he said.
Dr Asow also describes how drones tracked the movement of patients.
On 15 March 2025, people carrying the injured from the site of an attack to one of the temporary wards were followed by a hunting drone, he says.
It struck as they reached the gate of the building, killing nine people.
On 17 August of the same year a donkey cart was transferring patients to the alternative operating theatre because all the ambulances had been destroyed, Dr Asow told the BBC.
It was hit by a drone, killing two patients, the driver and the donkey, he says.
Image caption, The burnt-out ruins of a building in a compound that housed a medical warehouse and dialysis centre in el-Fasher pictured after it was hit by a missile
The RSF health minister dismissed these accounts as "a continuation of lies" by the military (SAF) and their local allies, known as the Joint Forces.
"Even the medics, and many of the people who were there, because of this conflict, these tribal issues, these racial issues, they are allied with the Joint Forces and with the SAF. That&#x27;s why you don&#x27;t depend on that narrative," he said.
Doctors told the BBC they believed RSF fighters deliberately targeted medical facilities because army or allied fighters were among their patients.
The onslaught of shelling and bombing culminated on 25 October 2025 with the RSF storming the hospital amid allegations of a massacre that killed more than 460 patients and their companions .
The RSF has denied a massacre occurred, although the WHO condemned the reported killings and satellite imagery appeared to show clusters of bodies on the blood-soaked grounds of the hospital.
The BBC shared details of its investigation with the UN human rights chief, who said our findings were "deeply disturbing", and consistent with the analysis of his office.
"Our monitoring and documentation shows the RSF repeatedly struck el-Fasher&#x27;s main hospitals and makeshift health facilities, destroying or seriously damaging them and severely impacting access to health services for thousands of civilians trapped in the city," TÃ¼rk said.
Healthcare in el-Fasher is now being run by the RSF-backed government. It has released several videos showing the Saudi Hospital and the Teaching Hospital re-opened, cleaned up and seeing some patients.
Dr Nugud says South Hospital is also set to reopen. He denied that doctors were forcibly abducted, telling the BBC that instead they had chosen to stay on to work.
"I have a video in front of the children&#x27;s hospital when we freed a consultant, a doctor and six of the medical staff who were working with the SAF, and then when el-Fasher got liberated, there was a forgiveness for all of them.
The BBC spoke to one of the doctors in the video, Dr Al-Haj Mohamed Ahmed. He told us he had no choice: "I was forced to be there.
"If I spoke out of line, they&#x27;d put a bullet through my head."
He was among several medical workers captured while trying to escape the city and sent back, along with Dr Mustafa Ahmed.
They distinguished themselves by bringing a cholera epidemic in a temporary prison at the paediatric hospital under control and were subsequently taken to Saudi Hospital.
"They started to say that you are the people of the city, and we take the city, so we need to work together for the sake of the city," says Dr Mustafa Ahmed, who describes waking up several mornings in a row at the prison to find 50 inmates dead of cholera.
A year on from the fall of el-Fasher, the trauma of this dark period in Sudan&#x27;s civil war is still painfully sharp.
The front lines have shifted elsewhere with no signs of a resolution to the conflict. And attacks on healthcare continue as part of a wider and devastating humanitarian crisis.
Maps and graphics by Miguel Roca-Terry, Jake Friend and Matt Mitchell-Camp

### 59. ★★★☆☆ The Federal Reserve's Latest October Inflation Forecast Has Arrived, and It's Terrible News for Wall Street - Yahoo Finance

- **分类**: 💰 全球金融
- **来源**: GN Fed

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

The Federal Reserve's Latest October Inflation Forecast Has Arrived, and It's Terrible News for Wall Street
Year four of Wall Street's bull market didn't disappoint. The artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure build-out lifted the Dow Jones Industrial Average (DJINDICES:^DJI), S&P 500 (SNPINDEX:^GSPC), and Nasdaq Composite (NASDAQINDEX:^IXIC) to several record highs throughout 2026.
But expecting the Dow, S&P 500, and Nasdaq Composite to head higher amid persistently elevated inflation is a tall task. Fed Chair Kevin Warsh and the Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) have quite the challenge on their hands in delivering price stability . Unfortunately, the latest inflation update for October hasn't done policymakers or Wall Street any favors.
Missed AI's "Act 1"? Act 2 Could Be 14x Bigger. Most investors think they missed the AI boat because they didn't buy Nvidia in 2005. But according to our analysts, we're only at the end of "Act 1"—the R&D phase. "Act 2" is the global rollout. Continue »
Fed Chair Kevin Warsh and the FOMC may raise rates multiple times in 2027. Image source: Official Federal Reserve Photo.
The Fed's October inflation update is a double whammy for stocks
Though there's no perfect inflation prediction tool, the Federal Reserve Bank of Cleveland's Inflation Nowcasting tool comes pretty close. The Cleveland Fed's tool provides daily forecasts for current- and forward-month inflation that are updated after the release of relevant economic data.
As of Oct. 20, forecasts for two key inflation measures were headed in the wrong direction.
For starters, we know the Consumer Price Index (CPI) reaccelerated to an annualized 3.7% in September. The Cleveland Fed's inflation tool predicts further acceleration to 3.77% in October. Rapidly rising fuel costs, caused by the ongoing closure of the Strait of Hormuz, likely explain why headline inflation is heading higher.
What's arguably even more worrisome is the projection for Core Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE). Core PCE excludes volatile food and energy costs, providing a comprehensive look at long-term price trends.
After three consecutive months of Core PCE standing pat at 3%, the Inflation Nowcasting tool predicts a steady reacceleration to 3.05% in September (Core PCE for September will be released on the 30th) and 3.13% in October.
The projected uptick in Core PCE suggests that the U.S. economy is dealing with more than just an energy supply shock. Businesses altering their shipping routes and shifting suppliers due to the Iran war are just some of the added costs now affecting the broader economy.
The odds of multiple FOMC rate hikes are climbing, and that's terrible news for stocks
With headline inflation topping the Fed's long-term 2% target for 67 months (and counting), Fed Chair Warsh hasn't minced his words about delivering price stability. On Sept. 16, Warsh and 11 other FOMC members voted unanimously to raise interest rates by a quarter point to 3.75%-4.00%.
With both headline inflation and Core PCE, the latter of which is one of the Fed's preferred price gauges, continuing to move in the wrong direction, the odds are increasing that policymakers will undertake several rate hikes over the next year.
According to the CME Group 's (NASDAQ:CME) proprietary FedWatch Tool, which uses 30-day Fed Funds futures to track the probability of changes to the federal funds target rate, there's a 72% probability that interest rates will rise by at least 75 basis points to 4.50%-4.75% by Sept. 15, 2027.
An aggressive Fed rate-hiking cycle would be awful news for a historically expensive, AI-driven bull market. If borrowing becomes costlier and the AI infrastructure build-out slows, even slightly, this could be enough of a catalyst to send a pricey stock market lower rather quickly.

Consider when Netflix made this list on December 17, 2004... if you invested $1,000 at the time of our recommendation, you'd have $350,756 !* Or when Nvidia made this list on April 15, 2005... if you invested $1,000 at the time of our recommendation, you'd have $1,363,905 !*
